Purely cosmetic, no notification. `components/groups/SeasonNameEditor.tsx` is the reusable client component behind it, used by the intermission "Edit" sheet (`SeasonSetupEditSheet`, the season about to start) and the "End season" confirmation sheet (last chance to name the one about to archive) — its idle-state trigger is a small `PencilIcon` button rather than underlined text, so it reads as an edit control rather than a stray link. The active season's own name, on the read-only `/settings` page itself, is edited from `GroupIdentitySheet` instead (a "Season name" field alongside the group's name and logo) rather than its own inline pencil-edit in the top identity row's meta line — one modal for the handful of "what is this thing called" decisions rather than a separate always-visible edit affordance for each.
  - Seeding still happens in exactly the same two places as before: first-time join, and season start (now for the *default-included* population above, not a literal opt-in list).
- **`delete_group` / `cancel_group_deletion`** — an owner-initiated delete is immediate, not grace-period. `delete_group` (owner-only) voids+refunds every non-terminal market in the group right away (via the same `_void_market()` helper `end_season()` uses), then drops the group row in the same call — every group-scoped table cascades from `groups.id`, so a plain `DELETE` is enough, no manual cleanup and no separate sweep. There's no undo once it's called. `groups.deletion_scheduled_at` and `cancel_group_deletion` still exist, but purely for the *other*, unrelated thing that uses them: the same grace-period mechanism fires automatically from two independent sweeps inside `expire_stale()`, both of which block new markets/members in the meantime (`create_market`/`join_group` both check the column) so a member can see it coming and the owner has a real window to `cancel_group_deletion` before the row actually goes:
  - **Intermission-specific, 5-day grace.** `group_deletion_scheduled_inactivity` fires when a seasons-enabled group sits in `intermission` for 30 days with nobody continuing — `start_season()` also clears the flag directly (and emits the ordinary `group_deletion_canceled`, since that really is what canceled it) as an alternative to calling `cancel_group_deletion` explicitly.
  - **General, any-group, 14-day grace, three reminders.** `group_deletion_notice_14d` fires for *any* group (seasons-enabled or not, in any season status) with no market created and no bet placed anywhere in it for 90 days, and nothing currently unresolved — the only path that covers a continuous (seasons-off) group, which never enters `intermission` and so could never be caught by the sweep above. Unlike that sweep, this one never force-voids anything: a group with any non-terminal market is left alone outright rather than assumed-empty, since 90 days of no *new* activity doesn't mean an existing open market has stopped mattering to whoever still has money on it. Two more pushes follow automatically if nobody cancels: `group_deletion_notice_7d` and `group_deletion_notice_1d`, one-shot stamped via `groups.deletion_reminder_7d_sent_at`/`deletion_reminder_1d_sent_at` (same pattern as `markets.closing_nudge_sent_at`) so a per-minute sweep can't re-fire either one. Both stamps get set immediately (to "already sent") by the intermission sweep above when *it* schedules a deletion, so its unrelated 5-day grace can never accidentally satisfy either reminder's "7/1 days left" window and misfire; `cancel_group_deletion` clears both stamps along with `deletion_scheduled_at`, so a group scheduled again later gets fresh reminders rather than finding them pre-marked sent.

  An owner clicking delete manually never touches `deletion_scheduled_at` at all — it's a completely separate code path that happens to still share `groups.deletion_scheduled_at`'s column and `cancel_group_deletion`'s undo, only for the two inactivity triggers above.
- **`react_to_market`** — one fixed emoji per person per market (`fire`/`laugh`/`clown`/`salute`/`thumbs_up`/`thumbs_down`), only ever on a `resolved`/`voided` market (gated by `is_market_visible()`, same choke point as everywhere else — a market's own subject can react too, once it resolves). Tapping the current pick again clears it; tapping a different one swaps it. No push notification. `lib/reactions.ts` holds the one canonical emoji/glyph mapping and display order, shared by the interactive picker (`ReactionBar`, on the reveal ticket) and a compact view-only facepile rendered on resolved/voided market rows/cards in the group hub — the latter shares its badge slot with the `needsAttention` "!" badge above, since a market can only ever be `open` (attention) or `resolved`/`voided` (reactions), never both.
- **Awards** (`group_titles`) — 4 persistent flavor badges (`lib/titles.ts`: The Oracle, Ice Cold, Degenerate, Risk Taker), surfaced on the Awards page (`AwardsRail`/`UnclaimedTitles`) and listed inline (icon, label, description, and the stat that earned it) on a holder's member profile page — no longer rendered as inline emoji flair next to every nickname on the leaderboard/profile (`Mention` used to take a `titles` prop for exactly that; it was removed, along with the `emoji` field on `lib/titles.ts`'s `TitleMeta`/`TitleBadge`, since a small unicode glyph next to dozens of names on a list added noise without adding information the Awards page doesn't already carry). Originally 8 — Bandwagon, Cursed, On Fire, and Whale were removed (`20260824100000_shrink_default_awards.sql`) when the 4 survivors became owner-renamable/re-iconable (see below) and those 4 couldn't be: Cursed/On Fire (streaks) and Bandwagon ("matches the market favorite") use bespoke window-function SQL that doesn't generalize into anything customizable, and leaving them as a permanently uncustomizable minority alongside 4 that could be renamed was worse than cutting them. Whale's dynamic ("most tokens wagered") already exists as a pickable custom-award shape, so a group that wants it back can add it themselves. All 4 are computed from real (never `voided`) bet/ledger history, restricted to still-active memberships. Oracle/Ice Cold/Degenerate (`_recompute_group_titles`) are comparative rankings across the whole group and only recompute every 3rd market that resolves (`group_settings.markets_resolved_since_titles`, bumped by `_bump_titles_counter` from inside `finalize_market`) — recomputing on every single resolution would flip a title back and forth on a couple of noisy bets. Risk Taker (`_upsert_risk_taker`) is the exception: it's monotonic (only a genuine new all-time-best payout multiple ever changes the holder, the same stat the `impressive_bet` push already tracks), so it updates live on every resolution with no flapping to guard against, and replaced the old standalone "most impressive bet" leaderboard card. A vacant title (nobody yet qualifies, e.g. under the 5-settled-bet minimum) is still a row with `user_id = null`, so the UI can show "unclaimed" rather than not knowing whether it's ever been computed. The leaderboard page's "Accuracy" stat reuses The Oracle's exact win-rate definition (bet's side/option matches the market's real outcome, min. 5 settled bets on `status = 'resolved'` markets) but isn't a stored `group_titles` row — it's computed ad hoc for every qualifying member each time the page loads, since it's a full ranking display, not a single-holder badge.

  **The 4 default titles are owner-renamable and re-iconable**, the same way a custom award's label/icon are configurable: `group_titles.label`/`icon_key` are nullable override columns (null = use `TITLE_META`'s code default), set via the owner-only `rename_group_title()` RPC (`lib/actions/titles.ts`'s `renameGroupTitle()`, `components/groups/EditTitleButton.tsx`'s pencil-icon modal, dropped onto every card/row a fixed title renders in). What's *not* customizable is the underlying dynamic itself or its description — an owner can call Oracle "The Prophet" with a different icon, but it still means highest win rate. `TITLE_META`'s `format()` function stays keyed to the fixed `TitleKey`, since the stat being formatted (a win rate, a bet count) doesn't change with the label.
- **Custom awards** (`custom_group_titles` / `custom_group_title_holders`) — a genuinely separate table from `group_titles`, not a generalization of it: the fixed 4's keys are a closed, code-defined set (`TITLE_ORDER`). A custom award instead picks one of **15 metrics** (`custom_title_metric` enum) crossed with a direction, giving an **18-option flat menu** (`lib/customAwards.ts`'s `CUSTOM_AWARD_SHAPES`) — win rate, bet count, tokens wagered, payout multiple, biggest single win/loss, all-time net, average bet size, distinct markets played, and counts of markets created/resolutions proposed/challenges raised/votes cast/reactions given/times a market subject, each a "rank everyone by an aggregate, take #1" query (`_compute_custom_title`) using the identical shape `_recompute_group_titles` already established for Oracle/Ice Cold/Degenerate. **Only 14 of those 18 are actually offered** (`AVAILABLE_CUSTOM_AWARD_SHAPES`, filtering out `win_rate_desc`/`win_rate_asc`/`bet_count_desc`/`payout_multiple_desc`): those 4 are exactly Oracle/Ice Cold/Degenerate/Risk Taker's own dynamics, and offering "Highest win rate" as a custom-award option when Oracle already exists on the same page would just be the same award twice. `create_custom_group_title` enforces this server-side too, not just in the menu — a duplicate `(metric, direction)` pair raises `invalid_operation`. Its icon (`custom_group_titles.icon_key`) is the same kind of choice as everything else award-shaped in this app — one of a fixed set (`lib/awardIcons.ts`'s `AWARD_ICONS`, same shape-checked `^[a-z0-9-]{1,32}$` text column as `groups.avatar_key`/`users.avatar_preset_key`), not free-typed, and the *same* set the 4 default titles pick from — `AwardGlyph` renders both from one shared icon key space rather than two parallel glyph systems. It used to be a raw emoji character in a column literally named `emoji`; `20260823130000_custom_award_icon_keys.sql` renamed the column and reworked `create_custom_group_title`'s validation once every other award-ish symbol in the app had already standardized on "pick from a fixed set," leaving free-typed emoji as the one inconsistent case. Owner-only, capped at 5 per group (`lib/limits.ts`'s `CUSTOM_AWARD_MAX_PER_GROUP`, bounding the added recompute cost), computed immediately on creation so the owner sees it reflect reality right away rather than waiting for the next batch. `_bump_titles_counter` recomputes custom titles on the exact same every-3rd-resolution cadence as the fixed 4 (one shared counter, not a second one to keep in sync), and reuses the existing `group_titles_updated` push rather than adding a new event type. Only `win_rate` and `avg_bet_size` keep the fixed titles' `having count(*) >= 5` floor — a rate a couple of noisy bets can spike — every count/sum/max-based metric has none, since a single real bet, vote, or market is the fact being reported, not an estimate of one. **`times_subject` is the one metric with a live privacy rule**: it only counts a `market_subjects` row on a `resolved`/`voided` market, mirroring `is_market_visible()` exactly — a market still `open` never contributes, even though `_compute_custom_title` itself runs with elevated privilege. Both `custom_group_title_holders.user_id` and `custom_group_titles.created_by` are `on delete set null`, matching `group_titles.user_id` and learned directly from the `notification_events.actor_id` incident above — a title holder or an award's creator deleting their account must never be blocked by either table.
- **`create_market_template` / `delete_market_template`** — the writes behind the template gallery and "save as template" (see the data-model entry above). `create_market_template(p_scope, p_group_id, p_title, p_description, p_market_type, p_options, p_unit)` mirrors `create_market()`'s own validation numbers exactly (140-char title, 2-10 `multiple_choice` options, 40-char option labels, unique/non-blank labels, a unit only for `over_under`) so a saved template can never later fail `create_market()` for something this step could have caught, rejects any option shaped like a resolved `@nickname` mention (that syntax is `create_market()`-only), counts literal `@` placeholders via the internal `_count_template_placeholders()` and rejects more than one, and caps saved templates at 30 per scope (`lib/limits.ts`'s `MARKET_TEMPLATE_MAX_PER_SCOPE`, the same bounding reasoning as `CUSTOM_AWARD_MAX_PER_GROUP`). `p_scope = 'curated'` is rejected outright — curated rows aren't created through this function at all, see "Notable design decisions" below. `delete_market_template` follows `delete_custom_group_title`'s exact existence-then-ownership shape, with no group-owner override to delete someone else's shared template.
- **`set_group_avatar`** — owner-only, sets/clears `groups.avatar_key`. 404s for a non-member before it ever checks ownership, same posture as every other group function.
- **`set_avatar_uploaded`** — flips `users.avatar_updated_at` for the caller's own row (to `now()`, or to `null` to clear), same reasoning as `set_notifications_enabled`: `users` has select and insert policies but no update policy, so a plain `update` would silently match zero rows. Also clears `avatar_preset_key` when uploading (`p_uploaded = true`) — a photo and a preset are mutually exclusive, see the data-model note above. The actual bytes live in the public `avatars` Storage bucket at a fixed, deterministic path (`{user_id}.jpg`, upserted on every re-upload), so there's no `avatar_path` column to keep in sync with it. `avatar_updated_at` alone answers "does this user have an uploaded photo" and doubles as the cache-busting query param on the rendered URL (`lib/userAvatars.ts`'s `userAvatarSrc()`).
- **`set_avatar_preset`** — the preset counterpart: sets/clears `users.avatar_preset_key` for the caller's own row and clears `avatar_updated_at` in the same update. `setAvatarPreset()` (`lib/actions/profile.ts`) is the one Server Action that calls it, from `AvatarPicker`'s icon grid.
- **`set_notifications_enabled` / `update_notification_categories` / `update_group_notification_prefs` / `update_marketing_email_opt_in`** — the writes behind `/profile/notifications` (see "Notification preferences" below). All four act on the caller's own row only, so there's nothing to authorize beyond membership. `update_marketing_email_opt_in` is the odd one out: it doesn't gate a push category like the other three, it's the later off-ramp for the marketing-email consent captured once at signup (see "Marketing email consent" below), with its own timestamp column for when the choice was last changed.
- **`accept_current_policy(p_version)`** — the one write behind `PolicyReapprovalGate` (see "Policy reapproval" below). Stamps the caller's `accepted_policy_version`/`accepted_policy_at`; the version itself always comes from the app's own `CURRENT_POLICY_VERSION` (`lib/actions/legal.ts`'s `acceptCurrentPolicy()` never lets the client supply it), so an out-of-date client can't accept-and-dismiss against a version that's no longer current.
- **`get_member_stats`** — the foundation for viewing another member's profile (`/groups/[groupId]/members/[membershipId]`, linked from leaderboard rows and the awards page's "held by" rows). Its record now also lists every award this member holds inline (built-in titles and custom awards together, via `lib/memberProfile.ts`'s unified `HeldAward` list) rather than a "Holds N awards" link off to the Awards page — see the design note on why. **This is a new, deliberate authority decision, not an extension of an existing gate**: today, within a group, only you can see your own accuracy/net/bet history — everyone else's is either invisible or (per `membership_ledger_net`'s own-rows-only limitation, see the money section above) silently reads as a real-looking zero. This function makes every non-`removed` member's performance record visible to every other currently-active member of the same group. Gated on `_caller_is_active_group_member()` (excludes both `removed` and `left` callers — a member who left can't browse anyone's profile any more, same as every other group surface), with the *target* allowed to be `left` as well as `active`/`dormant` (only `removed` is excluded), matching the leaderboard's existing precedent of still showing a left member's honest historical record if they actually played. Returns balance, tenure, accuracy (the same win-rate definition already shown on `/profile` for yourself, no min-bet threshold), lifetime tokens wagered, best call (highest payout/stake multiple), and **all-time net, read directly from `ledger` under this function's elevated privilege** — deliberately the first place the two known "silently reads zero for other people" gaps get a real, deliberate answer instead of an accidental blind spot. No new privacy exception for hidden-subject markets is needed: every stat is computed from `status = 'resolved'` bets (or the bettor's own bet rows regardless of market status for the wagered/settled-count figures), and a resolved market is already visible to everyone per `is_market_visible()`.
  - **`20260830130000`, prompted by `_prune_resolved_system_markets()`**: net is computed as `balance - sum(seed-reason ledger rows)` rather than summing every non-seed row — see the money section's note on why that's the identical number computed a way that survives pruning (seed rows are never deleted by it). Accuracy/tokens-wagered/settled-bet-count/best-call have no equivalent reformulation — they're genuinely gone once the underlying bets/markets are pruned, and a shrinking number is worse than no number. So for a pruned-pipeline-group membership specifically (matched by `is_public and name in ('NFL', 'CFB')` — the same two names `_prune_resolved_system_markets()` itself prunes, updated `20260906103000` when Sports split into NFL and CFB), all four come back `null` instead. `lib/memberProfile.ts`'s `hidesPipelineStats` flag carries that same check to the client, and `MemberProfileCard` drops those stat blocks from the layout entirely for those groups (net moves to a full-width row in their place) rather than rendering a permanent "—".
- **`get_head_to_head_markets`** — the second half of head-to-head analysis, called from `lib/actions/memberProfile.ts`'s `loadHeadToHead()` (the inline "Compare with someone" step on a member's page/modal) and, for a direct link, from the `/groups/[groupId]/members/[membershipId]/vs/[otherMembershipId]` fallback page directly. Adds no new disclosure beyond what `get_member_stats` already decided: same gate (`_caller_is_active_group_member`, both targets non-`removed` and in the same group), and every row comes from `status = 'resolved'` markets. Aggregates per `(market, user)` rather than joining bet rows directly, so a hedged bettor (hedging is opt-out, not the exception) collapses to one row per shared market instead of a cross-joined fan-out of every side/option combination the two of them happened to hold; the "choice" each side is shown as is resolved to a plain label in SQL (the bet side's text, or the option's label for multiple choice) rather than an enum or option id the client would have to look up itself. **Scoped to the group's current season** (`20260823150000`, corrected by `20260830200000`): the shared-markets query originally joined every resolved market either side had ever bet on, group-wide and all-time, which read as an all-time record even mid-season. The first attempt at scoping picked "most recent season by number, whatever its status" — wrong specifically during intermission, since `_finalize_season()` inserts the *next*, higher-numbered `intermission` row the instant the old one archives, so "most recent by number" resolved to that brand-new empty season and a comparison during intermission always came back with zero shared markets even though the season that just ended had real history. `v_season_id` now prefers whichever season is actually live (`active`, or `winding_down` while its last markets resolve) and only falls back to the most recently `archived` one — which during intermission is exactly the season that just ended — rather than trusting the number alone. A continuous (seasons-off) group has no `seasons` rows at all, so `v_season_id` stays null and the filter is a no-op, preserving all-time behavior for exactly the groups where "season" isn't a real boundary.
- **`get_group_join_message`** — a new member sees the owner's custom welcome message (`group_settings.join_message`, plain text, capped at 240 characters, written through `update_group_settings` like every other setting) in a modal the instant `JoinFlow` finishes joining them. Deliberately its own read function rather than a `join_group()` return-value change: `join_group` currently `returns setof memberships`, a real table type, and folding in a non-column field would mean switching to `returns table(...)` — a bigger, riskier signature change than a purely cosmetic feature warrants. Gated to an active member of the group (same 404-not-403 posture as everywhere else), but returns a bare `text` scalar rather than a row, so a non-member and a member-with-no-message-set are both indistinguishable `null` — there's no partial disclosure to worry about either way.
- **`group_settings.prize_text` / `punishment_text`** — free text describing what the champion gets and what the loser owes, same 120-character-capped, trim-to-null-if-empty handling as `join_message`, written through `update_group_settings`. Unlike `join_message` this needs no dedicated read function: it's meant to be visible at a glance to every member all the time (`StakesCard`, on the settings page and as a group-hub banner), not surfaced once at a particular moment, so the ordinary member-scoped `group_settings` select policy is all it needs. `_finalize_season` copies both into `season_results.snapshot` at the moment a season closes, alongside a new `loser` key (lowest-balance non-`removed` member, the mirror of `champion`) — see the data-model note on `season_results.snapshot` above for why that's a frozen copy rather than a live read.
- **`get_group_by_invite_code` / `_enforce_invite_code_rate_limit` / `_record_invite_code_miss`** — the invite-code preview a non-member gets on `/join/[code]` (group name, `accepting_members`, and the caller's own membership status, nothing about members, markets, or bets), plus the two internal helpers behind the 10-misses-per-hour guess limit it shares with `join_group`. Both helpers are revoked from `authenticated`; only those two functions call them. Both `get_group_by_invite_code` and `join_group` are also explicitly revoked from `anon` (`20260814120000_invite_functions_revoke_anon.sql`) — see the "notable design decisions" entry on the `anon` grant for why that revoke had to be explicit at all. See "Guessing at invite codes is rate limited in the database" above for why the miss paths stopped raising and why the preview is VOLATILE.
- **`invite_code_exists` / `_client_ip` / `_enforce_invite_code_ip_rate_limit` / `_record_invite_code_ip_miss`** — one of two functions in this app granted to `anon` (the other is `log_qr_scan`, directly below). See the data model section above for what it exists to do and how its IP-based limit differs from the account-based one.
- **`log_qr_scan(batch, platform, country, region)`** — the other `anon`-granted function, called by `barbets-www`'s `/go/[batch]` route (a separate repo, see "Domains and the mybarbets.com split") with nothing but the public anon key, since a card/NFC-tag scan happens before anyone has a session. Printed cards all carry the same fixed `/go/card` URL (reprinting isn't an option once cards are out), so `batch` is how location-specific NFC tags (`/go/rutgers`, `/go/wvu`, ...) are told apart from the generic printed run; `platform` is `android`/`ios`/`other`, and `country`/`region` come from Vercel's `x-vercel-ip-*` headers, both nullable since only Vercel's own edge network sets them. Writes to `qr_scans`, which like every other table here is RLS-enabled with zero policies — `log_qr_scan` is the only way in. No rate limiting, unlike the invite-code functions above: the worst a spammed call buys is junk analytics rows, not a secret or an account.
- **`list_qr_scan_totals()`** — the one read path into `qr_scans`, `is_platform_admin()`-gated same as `list_pipeline_health()` next to it, aggregated to one row per batch (total plus an android/ios/other breakdown, first/last scanned) rather than exposing raw rows. Surfaced on `/admin` via `QrScanTotalsCard`.
- **`touch_last_active()` / `record_signup()`** — the two writers behind `users.last_active_at` and the `signup` row in `lifecycle_events`, both acting on the caller's own identity only (`auth.uid()`), same as `set_notifications_enabled()`. `touch_last_active()` is called from `app/(app)/layout.tsx` on every authenticated request; its own `WHERE last_active_at is null or last_active_at < now() - interval '15 minutes'` is the entire debounce, so it's safe to call unconditionally. `record_signup()` is called from `signUp()`'s immediate-session branch and from `confirmSignup()` (both in `lib/actions/auth.ts`), never from plain sign-in, and is itself idempotent (`insert ... where not exists`) as a second line of defense against double-counting a signup.
- **`admin_signups_per_day` / `admin_wau_mau` / `admin_retention_cohorts` / `admin_lifecycle_event_totals` / `admin_group_stats`** — `admin.mybarbets.com`'s growth dashboards, `is_platform_admin()`-gated same as every other admin RPC. `admin_signups_per_day` reads `users.created_at` directly. **`admin_wau_mau`, `admin_retention_cohorts`, and `admin_group_stats` read a union of `memberships` (`joined_at`, covers both `group_create` and `group_join` since creating a group also inserts the creator's own membership), `markets` (`created_at`/`creator_id`), and `bets` (`created_at`/`user_id`) — not `lifecycle_events`** (`20260829180000`), since those three tables already have real history back to launch day and `lifecycle_events` only started recording the moment it was deployed; see the data-model section above for the incident this corrected. Only `admin_lifecycle_event_totals` and the `deleted_last_30d` half of `admin_group_stats` actually read `lifecycle_events`, for the two things it's still the right source for: a raw "is instrumentation firing" sanity check, and `group_delete`, which has no other reconstructible source once the group row is gone. `admin_retention_cohorts` cross-joins signup cohorts against week-offsets, a plain, unindexed cross join deliberately left unoptimized at current friend-group scale — no materialized view or pre-aggregation table until real usage says otherwise; every CTE column reference in it is fully qualified (`cohorts.cohort_week`, never a bare `cohort_week`) because PL/pgSQL implicitly exposes a function's `RETURNS TABLE` column names as variables inside its own body, and an unqualified reference sharing that name is ambiguous between "the variable" and "the query column" — `20260829200000` fixed exactly that after the bare-alias version raised `column reference "cohort_week" is ambiguous` on every real call, caught only once the function was actually invoked end-to-end as an authenticated admin rather than stopping at verifying its underlying joins directly. `admin_group_stats(p_active_days default 14)` defines "active group" as a **private** group (`is_public = false`) with both at least one market created and at least one bet placed in the trailing window — a market with nobody betting on it, or a bet without a market that week, doesn't count; a real market getting a real bet does. Scoped to private groups on purpose: NFL/CFB and campus/public groups are pipeline-driven or directory-joined, and counting them here would dilute the one thing this metric exists to answer — are actual friend groups still coming back. `scheduled_for_deletion` (`groups.deletion_scheduled_at is not null`) counts every group regardless of type, since deletion is a platform-wide operational fact, not a friend-group-engagement one. The existing `get_platform_admin_stats().active_groups` figure is unrelated and unchanged — it counts every group not scheduled for deletion, public and private alike, and is surfaced on the admin site as "Total groups" specifically so the two "active"-sounding numbers on the same page can't be confused for one another.
- **`_notification_category` / `_prefs_allow`** — internal helpers mapping an event type to one of `markets`/`results`/`admin`/`nudges`/`promos`/`other`, and deciding whether a given recipient's preferences let that category through. Both revoked from `authenticated`; only `get_event_recipients` calls them.
- **`send_nudges()`** — the second cron entry point, every 15 minutes (`barbets-nudges`), deliberately *not* folded into `expire_stale()`: everything in that function is load-bearing (markets closing, votes finalizing, seasons archiving, groups deleting), and these two nudges are flavor, so a bad query here can't throw partway through and leave a season un-archived. It emits two events and decides only *whether there's anything worth notifying about* — never who, which is entirely `get_event_recipients`' job.
  - **`weekend_nudge`**: Friday 12:00-12:15 in the group's *own* `group_settings.timezone` (which until now was a pure display caption). Only for groups that could act on it (betting currently open, not in intermission, not scheduled for deletion, at least 2 active members) and only for **quiet** ones: nothing currently open, and nothing created in the last 3 days. A group already mid-conversation doesn't need prodding. Deduped to one per group per 5 days against `notification_events` itself, which also covers an owner changing the timezone mid-week. Group-scoped with no `market_id` (that's the point), so it joins the `notification_events_market_events_have_market` allow-list.
  - **`market_closing_soon`**: an `open` market inside 2 hours of `closes_at`. `markets.closing_nudge_sent_at` is stamped once so it can never fire twice for the same market (the same one-shot pattern as `closed_at`), and `distinct on (group_id)` plus a 24-hour lookback caps it at one per group per day, so a group closing four markets at once costs a lapsed member one push rather than four. That cap is per *group* rather than per recipient on purpose: the recipient set is the same lapsed-member set either way, which makes the cheap check the correct one and avoids a per-user delivery log.
- **`expire_stale()`** — the cron entry point, scheduled via pg_cron every minute (`barbets-expire-stale`, same cadence as send-push below — tightened from an original 5-minute interval once send-push proved 1-minute pg_cron scheduling works fine on this project's plan). Covers 24h (or closes_at) unsponsored expiry, market auto-close at `closes_at` (stamping `closed_at`) — **except for a market that reaches `closes_at` with literally zero bets ever placed, which gets voided outright via `_void_market()` instead of closing**, since there's nothing for anyone to propose/challenge/vote on and it would otherwise sit in `closed` forever waiting on a proposal nobody had a reason to make; that auto-void is deliberately silent (no `market_closed`, no `market_voided`), the same "nothing happened here" convention the unsponsored expiry already uses, and only the natural sweep is affected (a human calling `propose_resolution` on a zero-bet market is still a deliberate action and untouched) — **except a `markets.is_system_market` row is never auto-voided this way regardless of its bet count**, added by `20260828100000` once zero-bet Sports markets were confirmed getting voided before their own resolve Edge Function ever got a chance to run (`expire_stale()` is cron'd every minute, `sports-resolve-markets` only every 30 at the time — the void nearly always won the race). A system market still transitions to `closed` on schedule and gets a real outcome from `_resolve_system_market()` regardless of whether anyone bet on it, since the whole point of the pipeline is recording the real-world result either way. `is_system_market` is a dedicated boolean, not an inference from `creator_id is null` — `creator_id` is nullable for an unrelated reason (`20260823100000`'s `ON DELETE SET NULL`), so a perfectly ordinary member-created market ends up with a null `creator_id` the moment its creator deletes their account, which would have made auto-void skip it too — no-challenge auto-finalize and vote-window auto-finalize (both against each group's own `resolution_window_hours`), the `winding_down` hard-cap sweep (force-void anything still `proposed`/`disputed` past `wind_down_deadline`, then archive), timed season auto-end (a plain `seasons.ends_at <= now()` check, not recomputed from `group_settings` — see above), the 30-day-abandoned-intermission sweep (schedules group deletion the same way `delete_group()` does), the general 90-day-any-group-inactivity sweep plus its two follow-up reminder sweeps (see the `delete_group`/`cancel_group_deletion` bullet above for the full mechanism), the grace-period group-deletion sweep, and the timed season auto-end. **Every loop body is wrapped in its own `begin ... exception ... end` block**, which in PL/pgSQL is an implicit savepoint: a raise on one row rolls back that iteration only and the loop continues. Before `20260813180000` there was no error handling at all, and since pg_cron's `select expire_stale();` is a single transaction over every group on the platform, one bad row rolled back the entire run and then did it again every minute forever — see "Notable design decisions." A failed iteration is recorded in `sweep_failures` instead (the write survives, since only the inner savepoint rolled back), and `send-push` posts one Slack card per stuck row. The row-locking loops use `for update skip locked` so a market a member is actively acting on is skipped this minute rather than blocking all nine sweeps for everyone. `get_cron_job_status()` (service_role only) reports every `barbets-*` job's registered schedule for debugging (there are four now: `barbets-expire-stale`, `barbets-send-push`, `barbets-nudges`, and `barbets-purge-notifications`), since `cron.job` isn't exposed via PostgREST directly.
- **`_end_season(p_group_id, p_actor_id)` / `end_season(p_group_id)`** — the season-ending work, and the authorization gate in front of it. They are separate because `end_season()` reads its caller from `auth.uid()` and requires them to be the group's owner, which means it could never be called by cron: `auth.uid()` is NULL there, so the membership check matched nothing and it raised `not_found`. `expire_stale()` had been calling it for every season past `ends_at` since `20260719147000`, so timed seasons never auto-ended *and* that raise aborted the whole platform's sweep. `_end_season()` takes its actor as a parameter (NULL from cron, which is exactly what `_finalize_season` already expects for a season nobody ended) and asks no authorization questions; `end_season()` is now nothing but the not_found/forbidden checks plus a delegation. Covered by `tests/integration/lifecycle.season_auto_end.test.ts`, which is the first test in the suite to use a non-`manual` season length — every other one leaves `ends_at` NULL and never reaches that loop, which is why this went unnoticed.
- **`purge_processed_notification_events()`** — the 30-day retention cleanup of already-`processed_at` `notification_events` rows (that table otherwise has no retention policy; nothing queries events by age, but there's no reason to keep them forever), plus `sweep_failures` rows untouched for 30 days. Its own daily cron entry (`barbets-purge-notifications`, 03:17 UTC) since `20260813180000`, having previously been folded into `expire_stale()` — see "Notable design decisions" for why that moved.
- **`group_members_who_played(p_group_id, p_membership_ids)`** — which of those memberships have any non-seed `ledger` entry, for the leaderboard's "does this departed member still belong on the board?" test. It exists because `ledger_select_own` is own-rows-only, so the page cannot answer that question about anyone but the viewer (see the design note). It is deliberately the *narrowest* function that answers it: **membership ids only, never an amount, a count, or a date**, gated on `_caller_is_active_group_member()`, and matching `m.group_id = p_group_id` per row so a caller can't probe membership ids belonging to another group. The one bit it discloses ("this person played") is already what their row on the leaderboard says out loud. A function returning someone else's *net* or weekly swing would be a different decision and this is not a precedent for it.
- **`_record_sweep_failure()` / `claim_unreported_sweep_failures()` / `get_sweep_failures()`** — the `sweep_failures` plumbing. `_record_sweep_failure` is called from inside `expire_stale()`'s exception handlers and upserts with an attempt counter rather than inserting, since a permanently stuck row is retried every minute and one row per minute would bury its own signal. `claim_unreported_sweep_failures` marks and returns in one statement, the same claim pattern as `claim_notification_events` and for the same reason. `get_sweep_failures()` is a service_role read-only diagnostic, same idea as `get_cron_job_status()`. `_record_sweep_failure` also carries an explicit `grant ... to service_role` (`20260826150000`) on top of its original revokes: every existing caller was another `SECURITY DEFINER` function calling it internally (running as the function owner, which always has execute), but the Sports pipeline calls it directly from a service-role Edge Function, with no enclosing sweep function to inherit privilege from.
- **`pipeline_settings` / `set_pipeline_enabled()` / `list_pipeline_settings()`** — the admin-console kill switch for the Sports pipeline, one row (`enabled`, seeded `false` so nothing posts a real market until deliberately turned on). Follows `app_admins`'s convention for a small, platform-scope table — RLS on, zero policies, only reachable through the two `is_platform_admin()`-gated functions (same shape as `send_admin_broadcast`) or a service-role read. Each Edge Function checks its own row before doing anything else, via the same service-role client pattern `send-push` already uses to read `group_settings.resolution_window_hours` — no new plumbing, a Server Action's RPC write and an Edge Function's direct table read meet only at the row itself. `AdminPipelineTogglesForm` doesn't flip the checkbox straight through: both directions open a confirmation `Modal` first (same component `ForfeitModeratorButton` uses) naming the actual consequence — turning on starts real external API calls and real market creation, turning off leaves anything already in flight unresolved until it's back on — since a bare checkbox made both of those one accidental click away.
- **`pipeline_runs` / `_record_pipeline_run()` / `list_pipeline_health()`** — added by `20260828110000` because `sweep_failures` alone can't tell "quiet because nothing's wrong" apart from "quiet because the pipeline stopped running" (a misconfigured cron entry or an Edge Function that stopped deploying leaves `sweep_failures` empty too, which reads as healthy). Each pipeline Edge Function calls `_record_pipeline_run(pipeline, job, succeeded, failed)` once per invocation, right before returning its response — an append-only log, one row per run, service_role-only same as `_record_sweep_failure()`. `pipeline_runs.job`'s check constraint (`20260906102000`) lists `'create'`/`'resolve'`/`'weekly_prepare'`/`'weekly_publish'` (`'create'` now unused, left over from before sports moved from a `create`/`resolve` pair to `weekly_prepare`/`weekly_publish`/`resolve` when it became a weekly pipeline — see the Phase 2 section below). `list_pipeline_health()` (`is_platform_admin()`-gated, same shape as `list_pipeline_settings()`) returns one row per `(pipeline, job)` — 3 total (sports' weekly_prepare/weekly_publish/resolve) — joining each job's most recent `pipeline_runs` row against a rollup of whatever's currently sitting in `sweep_failures` for that job's sweep name. The admin panel turns that into a badge per pipeline: **Failing** if any of its jobs has an open `sweep_failures` row, **Stale** if any job's last run is older than its own threshold (or has never run at all), **Healthy** otherwise, **Off** if the kill switch itself is off. The staleness thresholds are a client-side constant (`STALE_AFTER_MINUTES` in `AdminPipelineTogglesForm.tsx`, a plain map from job to minutes now that there's only the one pipeline), not read from the schedule migrations, so keep the two in sync if a cron interval ever changes — sports' two weekly jobs specifically need a days-scaled threshold rather than the hours-scaled one every other job uses, since a job that only runs once a week is expected to go quiet for days between runs.

- **`game_of_week_picks` / `list_game_of_week_picks()` / `pick_game_of_week()`** (`20260906101000`) — the weekly NFL/CFB Game of the Week pipeline's own state, one row per `(league, week_key)`. `sports-weekly-prepare` inserts a row per league every Monday with that week's Odds API candidates (`[{event_id, home, away, commence_time}]`, soonest-kickoff first); an admin picks one from `/admin/game-of-the-week` via `pick_game_of_week(p_pick_id, p_event_id)` (`is_platform_admin()`-gated, validates the event id is actually one of that row's own candidates, rejects a pick once `status = 'published'`); `sports-weekly-publish` turns the pick — or, absent one, its own fallback — into the real market every Tuesday. Same "RLS on, zero client policies" shape as `pipeline_settings`/`pipeline_runs`. `chosen_by` is left `null` specifically when the eventual pick was the fallback rather than an admin's actual choice, so the past-picks history can tell the two apart. `market_id` is `on delete set null`, not cascade — see `_prune_resolved_system_markets()`'s entry above for why this table's own history has to outlive the market row it points at. `list_game_of_week_picks()` (`is_platform_admin()`-gated, same shape as `list_pipeline_settings()`) returns every row, current week first per league — the admin console's picker page and its past-weeks history are both just this one call. See the Phase 2 section below for the full weekly cadence this feeds.

## Money & the ledger

`ledger` is an append-only, signed log (`seed` / `bet` / `payout` / `refund`) — no client can ever UPDATE or DELETE it. Every balance change writes a matching ledger row in the same transaction that changes `memberships.balance`, so the ledger is always independently reconstructable and auditable. **The one exception**: `ledger.market_id` cascades on delete (`20260830120000`), solely so `_prune_resolved_system_markets()` can drop old auto-generated markets past each pipeline group's own retention cap — see that function's entry above. A deleted ledger row doesn't touch `memberships.balance`, so nobody's real balance is affected. **Net survives this for free**: since `balance` always equals `sum(all ledger rows)` for a membership including its seed row(s), `net = balance - sum(seed-reason rows)` is mathematically identical to summing every non-seed row directly, and seed rows are structurally immune to the cascade (`market_id` is always null on them, per `ledger_seed_has_no_market_or_bet`). `get_member_stats()` computes net this way for every group, not just the pipeline ones — it's a strict improvement, not a special case. No other group's ledger history is ever touched by the cascade itself.

**"All-time net" is one figure with one definition, and it comes from a view, not a client-side sum.** Every ledger entry except the `seed` itself (so reseeding for a new season doesn't read as "winning" tokens back). `membership_ledger_net` (`20260813190000`) aggregates it in Postgres and is what the groups hub, `/profile`, and the leaderboard's all-time card all read:

```sql
create view membership_ledger_net with (security_invoker = true) as
  select m.id as membership_id, m.user_id, m.group_id,
         coalesce(sum(l.amount), 0)::bigint as net,
         count(l.id)::bigint as entry_count
  from memberships m
  left join ledger l on l.membership_id = m.id and l.reason <> 'seed'
  group by m.id, m.user_id, m.group_id;
```

`entry_count` distinguishes "no activity" from "activity that happens to net to zero", which a bare sum cannot. Note that it is subject to the same own-rows-only limit as `net` below, so it can only answer that question about the viewer themselves. **The cross-member version of the question goes through `group_members_who_played()`**, not through this view.

**The zero this view returns for other people is not their real net.** `ledger_select_own` is own-rows-only, and `security_invoker = true` means the aggregate runs under the caller's own RLS, so asking for another member's row yields `net = 0, entry_count = 0`. That is deliberate and matches what the client-side sums it replaced already produced, but it is a number that looks real and isn't. Anything that genuinely needs another member's net must go through a SECURITY DEFINER function and decide, explicitly, that exposing it is intended. See the design-decisions note for the two call sites currently living on that zero without meaning to.

## The market page template

`app/(app)/groups/[groupId]/markets/[marketId]/page.tsx` renders one of four screens off `markets.status`. Three of them share a single template with a **fixed section order**; the point of the fixed order is that a member who has learned one market type has learned all of them, so nothing moves when the type changes.

Shared chrome on every one: the back link carries the *group's* name, the status pill and the `···` overflow (owner void, or the creator fallback when the owner is a subject) sit beside it, and a dark `PoolStrip` sits under the title. Three cells everywhere, with exactly one exception: an **open market carrying a `bonus_pool` takes a fourth Bonus cell** (`Pool / Bonus / Bets / Closes`, per-cell `flex` weights, a `bg-honey-500/12` wash behind Bonus, and the third label shortened from "Closes in" to "Closes" to fit). The over/under line still never gets a cell — it lives in `LineTicket`. See the design-decisions note below for why the bonus reversed and the line didn't.

A cell whose figure is tappable passes a client component as its `value` (`ClosesInValue` → the exact close time, `BonusPoolValue` → the bonus explainer) rather than an `onPress` handler, which is what keeps `PoolStrip` itself renderable from a server component. Both use the same affordance on the dark strip: `underline decoration-dotted decoration-espresso-300`.

| status | screen | strip cells | the one action |
|---|---|---|---|
| `pending_sponsor` | endorsement | Endorse by / Betting runs / Table | pinned "Endorse it" + "Not now" bar |
| `open` | the five-slot template | Pool / Bets / Closes in, or Pool / Bonus / Bets / Closes with a `bonus_pool` | pinned bet slip |
| `proposed` | the proposed call | Pool / Bets / Final in | "Challenge this call", in slot 4 |
| `closed` | waiting on a result | Pool / Bets / Status | "Propose the outcome", in slot 4 |
| `disputed` | the secret ballot | Pool / Bets / Vote ends | the ballot itself |

Every stage's action goes through `ResolutionTimeline`'s `children`, including on `closed` — `ProposeResolutionCard` has no card, copy, or divider of its own and only varies its label ("Propose result early" while betting is still open, since proposing is what closes it; "Propose the outcome" once it already has). It used to render its own dashed "waiting on a result" card above the timeline, which put the button in a different place on `closed` than on `open` and restated what the timeline's first step already said.

The five slots, in order: **(1)** your position (`PositionTicket`), only when you have money down; **(2)** otherwise the explainer — `LineTicket` for over/under, `OptionsTicket` for multiple choice, and *nothing* for yes/no, where the question is already the explanation; **(3)** `HowItSettlesCard`, criteria plus the started-by/endorsed-by/hidden-from chips, with `ClarificationRequests`' "?" in its corner; **(4)** `ResolutionTimeline`, whose `children` are the stage's one action under a divider; **(5)** `BetslipBar`, pinned above `BottomNav`, the only place a stake is ever entered.

`TicketCard` is the outlined shell slots 1 and 2 share with the endorsement screen's `VouchingTicket` and the proposed screen's `ProposedOutcomeTicket`. Exactly one of them is ever on screen at a time — that is what makes the heavier outline mean "this is the thing you're deciding about" rather than being decoration. On `proposed` that means the viewer's own position renders *inside* `ProposedOutcomeTicket` rather than in a ticket below it (`PositionRows` is shared by both): what a call is worth to you is not a separate fact from the call, it's the first thing anyone reads it for, and two cards made you look in two places to answer one question.

Two decisions here are load-bearing enough to have their own entries in "Notable design decisions": why slots 1 and 2 exclude each other and why the stage's action never sits where betting does, and why the drawn payout projection was dropped rather than built (short version: it would have repealed sealed odds).

**Banded modals.** Several modals own their own edge-to-edge header/footer bands instead of `Modal`'s default padded stack: the bonus pool explainer (`BonusPoolValue`), its group-level twin (`PendingBonusPoolNote`, same band and the same two-row arithmetic block for money that has no market yet), "Call the result" (`ProposeResolutionCard`, two steps under one band that reads `Step 1 of 2` / `Step 2 of 2`), owner void (`MarketOverflowMenu`'s `VoidAction`, also two steps under one band), the full ledger (`SettlementLedger`), and `CompareMemberPicker` (a "Compare with" picking step and a "Head to head" comparing step under one band, `Back` between them rather than a step counter, since going back changes what's being asked rather than advancing a fixed sequence). They share the band styling with `TicketCard` on purpose — `bg-espresso-50 px-[18px]`, uppercase label left, meta (or nothing) right — so a modal reads as the same object family as the cards behind it. `Modal` takes `padded={false}` plus a `panelClassName` for this rather than being forked; every other modal in the app still gets the default `space-y-3 p-5`. `RouteModal` (the intercepted-route shell, a different component from `Modal` — see above) uses the same band for its own close control rather than the floating top-right button it used to have, so an intercepted-route dialog and a locally-opened one read as the same family too.

"Review your market" uses the same band and the same espresso outline but is **no longer a modal** — it's step 3 of the create-market wizard (`ReviewTicket` in `MarketForms.tsx`), a full page like the two steps before it. It is still where the endorsement rule lives (it used to be small print under the create form's submit button, explaining the consequence of an action nobody had taken yet), and it still only appears when `group_settings.require_endorsement` is actually on, since "betting opens as soon as you create it" is the unremarkable default and doesn't need a highlighted callout. This same step also carries a secondary "Save as template" action next to "Create market" (`SaveAsTemplateModal`) — independent of submission, so saving a template neither requires nor triggers actually creating the market.

Two of them (`ProposeResolutionCard`, `VoidAction`) list the consequences of confirming as `ConsequenceRow` (`components/ui/ConsequenceRow.tsx`): coloured dots strung on the same connecting rail `ResolutionTimeline` draws, because these are steps in order, not bullets. The dot colour ranks them — danger for what happens to other people immediately, dark for the window they get to react in, faint for the eventual end.

**After a bet lands**, `BetslipBar`'s `BetConfirmedOverlay` takes the whole screen: a torn ticket stub carrying stake, side, market title, group, a live `CountdownTimer`, and the balance left. It still shows no odds or projected payout, same rule as the slip itself. Its one button goes to the group's market list, not back to this market — the bet is placed and the odds stay sealed, so there is nothing left on the market page to come back for.

## Server Actions & error handling

Every mutating action in `lib/actions/*.ts` calls a Postgres RPC and returns an `ActionResult<T>`:

```ts
type ActionResult<T> = { data: T; error?: undefined } | { data?: undefined; error: string };
```

**This is load-bearing, not a style choice.** Next.js redacts any error *thrown* out of a Server Action in production — the client only ever sees a generic message + a digest, never the real text, even for an expected rejection like "betting is not open on this market." Actions must catch the Postgres error and *return* it instead; client components check `result.error` rather than wrapping the call in try/catch. `lib/errors.ts`'s `runRpc()` does this conversion and also strips the internal `invalid_operation:` / `not_found:` style prefix so the user sees plain text, not what looks like an error code.

Read-only Server Components query `visible_markets` and read-only RPCs directly — those still throw/404 via `notFoundIfEmpty()`, since that path isn't subject to the same Server Action redaction.

There is exactly one **read-only Server Action**, `loadMoreSettledMarkets()` in `lib/actions/feed.ts`, behind the group feed's "Load more" button. It calls no RPC, but it still returns an `ActionResult<T>` for the reason above: the redaction rule is about *how a Server Action fails*, not about whether it mutates, and the button needs copy it can actually render. It does no membership check of its own — `visible_markets` is the choke point, and a caller who isn't in the group gets an empty page, indistinguishable from having reached the end of the list.

## Signup abuse protection

Starting 2026-08-10, a bot began hitting the public signup form continuously: confirmed email,
one sign-in at the moment of account creation, never seen again, ~15-25 accounts a day. By
2026-08-26, 271 of the app's accounts fit that exact pattern and were removed with
`scripts/cleanup-spam-accounts.mjs` (see the "Project structure" tree above). Signup now has two
independent layers against a repeat:

1. **Cloudflare Turnstile**, wired into every form that sends mail through `supabase.auth` -
   `SignUpForm`, `SignInForm`, `ForgotPasswordForm`, and `ConfirmEmailForm`'s resend button
   (`resendSignupCode`) (`components/auth/TurnstileField.tsx`). Supabase's captcha toggle
   (Authentication > Attack Protection > Enable CAPTCHA protection) is all-or-nothing across sign
   up, sign in, password recovery, and OTP resend, not something you can enable per endpoint, so
   all of these carry the widget even though only sign-up was ever the target. `confirmSignup`
   (the code-entry submit itself) is the one exception - `verifyOtp` only checks a code against an
   email that already passed the gated `signUp()` call, it never sends anything, so there's
   nothing here for Turnstile to protect. `TurnstileField` renders with
   `appearance: 'interaction-only'`, so it takes no space and shows nothing to a legitimate
   visitor; Cloudflare only surfaces a visible challenge when its risk signals actually call for
   one. The widget's own hidden `cf-turnstile-response` input rides along in the form's normal
   `FormData`, so the Server Actions in `lib/actions/auth.ts` just read
   `formData.get('cf-turnstile-response')` and pass it through as `options.captchaToken` - no
   client-side token plumbing needed. The Turnstile secret key lives only in the Supabase
   dashboard, never in this repo; `NEXT_PUBLIC_TURNSTILE_SITE_KEY` (public by design) is the only
   half that's an env var here. **The all-or-nothing part reaches past this repo too**:
   `barbets-admin`'s sign-in form (`admin.mybarbets.com`) calls the same project's
   `signInWithPassword`, so it needs the identical widget/`captchaToken` wiring or every admin
   login fails with "captcha protection: request disallowed" - discovered live when that site's
   login form first shipped without it. Any future app added against this Supabase project
   inherits the same requirement for every `supabase.auth` call it makes, not just the ones that
   send mail.

2. **New accounts are app-only - built, but not yet switched on.** `checkSignupFromApp()` in
   `lib/actions/auth.ts` rejects `signUp()` in production unless the request's `user-agent` header
   contains `BarbetsApp`, a marker `capacitor.config.ts` appends via `appendUserAgent` - only the
   native app's WebView sets it, since `app.mybarbets.com` is a normal public URL any browser can
   also load directly. This is a soft gate, not a security boundary: a script can set any user
   agent it likes, so it stops the "visit the page, fill the form" path a bot takes, not a
   determined attacker who specifically targets it. Turnstile above is what actually has to hold.
   It's gated behind `APP_ONLY_SIGNUP_ENABLED` (unset/anything but `"true"` = off) as well as the
   same `VERCEL_ENV`-first production check `lib/errorReporter.ts` uses, and ships **disabled**:
   turning it on before a native build carrying the UA marker has cleared both app stores would
   lock out every existing install, not just browser visitors, since none of them send the marker
   yet. Flip `APP_ONLY_SIGNUP_ENABLED=true` in Vercel once that rollout is far enough along. A web
   visitor who fails the check (once enabled) sees an error directing them to `/download` on the
   marketing site rather than a dead end.

**The Confirm signup email is code-only, no link at all**, matching `ConfirmEmailForm` being the
only way to finish signing up (a link-first version of this email tested worse: tapping it from
the Mail app opens a fresh, contextless tab straight into a signed-in `/demo`, while the tab the
person actually signed up in is still sitting on the code-entry screen - it reads as having landed
somewhere wrong rather than as confirmation succeeding). This lives entirely in the Supabase
dashboard (Authentication > Email Templates > Confirm signup), not in git - there's no local copy
to diff against, so if the email ever reverts to showing a confirmation link, it was changed back
(or reset to the Supabase default) directly in the dashboard. The Reset Password template is the
opposite case and still needs its link, since password recovery has no code-entry UI (see
"Password recovery uses `verifyOtp`, not PKCE code exchange" under "Notable design decisions worth
remembering" below) - its link `href` still needs the `/auth/confirm` customization,
`{{ .SiteURL }}/auth/confirm?token_hash={{ .TokenHash }}&type=recovery&next=/reset-password`
instead of the default `{{ .ConfirmationURL }}`, or clicking it verifies against Supabase's own
hosted endpoint and redirects with the session in a URL fragment this app's server-rendered pages
never see, landing signed-out on the splash with no sign anything happened. See the design-decision
note just referenced for why. `app/auth/confirm/route.ts` still accepts `type=signup` too, purely
for anyone with an old link-carrying confirmation email still sitting unread in their inbox from
before the link was removed.

**The code is however many digits the project's Authentication > Emails > "OTP Length" setting
says, not a Barbets choice** (6 as of this writing) - `ConfirmCodeBoxes`' `CONFIRM_CODE_LENGTH`
hardcodes that same number of boxes, since there's no runtime way for the client to ask Supabase
how long the code it just emailed is. `supabase/config.toml`'s `[auth.email] otp_length` mirrors it
for local dev parity, and the two were already caught drifting once (project default of 8 in
production against a stale `6` locally, before the project was deliberately changed to 6 for
shorter/faster entry). If the dashboard setting is ever changed again, `CONFIRM_CODE_LENGTH` needs
changing to match, or nobody can ever fill the last box (too long) or the boxes fill with a stray
leftover digit (too short) and "Confirm email" never enables. Changing the dashboard setting itself
has to happen by hand too - it's one field among many in the project's live auth config, and
`supabase config push` pushes that whole file, clobbering unrelated settings intentionally
different between local and production (see "Signup abuse protection" above).

## Notifications

`notification_events` is an internal queue table (no RLS policies at all — only `service_role` touches it) written transactionally by whichever Postgres function causes a notifiable transition. This is deliberate: several transitions (market auto-close, auto-finalize, auto-end-season) only ever happen via the `expire_stale()` cron job, so hooking notifications into Next.js Server Actions would silently miss most of them. Writing the event *inside* the same Postgres transaction that changed the state means "this happened, exactly once" is transactionally safe, decoupled from "send a push" (network I/O, retryable).

`get_event_recipients(event_id)` is the single choke point for who gets notified: it reuses the same subject-exclusion logic as `is_market_visible`, filters to members with a live `push_subscriptions` row and `notifications_enabled = true`, applies the recipient's own per-category/per-group preferences via `_prefs_allow` (see "Notification preferences" below), and excludes the triggering actor (`actor_id`) if there is one. Notably, `finalize_market()`'s actor is just `auth.uid()` — when a voter's ballot auto-finalizes the market in the same request, that's still their session, so they're correctly excluded from the "market resolved" push about their own action; cron-triggered finalizes have no request context, so `auth.uid()` is null and nobody is excluded on that basis. `betting_opened` and `season_ended` are group-scoped (every non-removed member, actor excluded) rather than market-scoped. `impressive_bet` repurposes `actor_id` to mean the opposite of everywhere else: the one person to notify, not the one to exclude — `finalize_market()` sets it to the bettor whose payout just became the group's new all-time-best payout multiple (`payout ÷ amount`, see `get_most_impressive_bet()`), and only they get the push. `member_joined` is also group-scoped but single-recipient like `impressive_bet` — its one recipient is always `groups.owner_id`, not the actor, so a group with a large roster doesn't get a push blast every time someone joins; only `join_group()`'s genuinely-new-membership paths emit it, not a dormant member's rejoin. `market_voided` is market-scoped like `market_resolved` and joins it in the subject-inclusion branch (`p_include_subjects`) for the same reason: voiding lifts `is_market_visible()`'s privacy gate just like resolving does, so subjects are very much part of the intended audience, not excluded from it.

**`notification_events.actor_id` is `on delete set null`** (`20260822160000_notification_events_actor_fk_set_null.sql`), not the Postgres default of `no action`. It used to have no `on delete` clause at all, which silently blocked deleting a user's account if any notification event — however old, however long since processed — still referenced them as the actor: `public.users.id` cascades from `auth.users` on delete, and that cascade hit this FK the moment it reached `notification_events`, failing `deleteAccount()`'s `admin.auth.admin.deleteUser()` call for anyone who had ever sponsored a market, proposed a resolution, or challenged one within the 30-day retention window. `get_event_recipients` already treats a null `actor_id` as "nobody to exclude," the same as a cron-triggered event, so nulling it on account deletion is a real, already-handled case rather than a new one. Found via a CI flake investigation that turned out to be unrelated — the test suite's own cleanup hit the identical error but silently logs and swallows it instead of failing, which is why this went unnoticed rather than pointing straight at itself.

**`markets.creator_id` and `markets.sponsor_id` are also `on delete set null`** (`20260823100000_markets_creator_sponsor_fk_set_null.sql`) — the same bug, found immediately after fixing the one above: deleting a user who had sponsored a market still failed, now on `markets_sponsor_id_fkey` instead. Unlike `notification_events`' 30-day retention window, a market has no purge sweep at all, so this one had no expiry — anyone who had ever created or sponsored a single market, in any group, ever, could not self-delete their account. `creator_id` was `not null`, which `on delete set null` cannot honor directly (a `not null` column can never receive the `null` an `on delete set null` action would try to write), so it had to become nullable first; `HowItSettlesCard`'s "Started by" chip already renders conditionally (`{creator && <SettlesChip>...}`), so a market whose creator has since deleted their account already degrades to simply not showing that chip. **A broader audit for the same bug class turned up five more columns with the identical gap, all now fixed the same way** (`20260823110000_remaining_user_fk_set_null.sql`): `bets.user_id`, `votes.voter_id`, `challenges.challenger_id`, `resolution_proposals.proposer_id`, and `resolution_clarifications.requester_id` are all now `on delete set null` (each had to lose its `not null` first, same reasoning as `creator_id` above). `bets.user_id` was likely the highest-impact of the whole set, since it's set on every bet a user ever places, not just on the much rarer creator/sponsor role. The audit's sixth candidate, `market_subjects.user_id`, turned out to already be `on delete cascade` from its original migration — no fix needed there, and worth remembering exactly because it's part of a composite primary key (`market_id`, `user_id`), which would have ruled out `on delete set null` outright had it needed fixing. See the "notable design decisions" note on this incident for the full audit and reasoning.

`claim_notification_events(p_limit)` is how a run takes work off the queue. It marks the oldest `p_limit` unprocessed rows processed and returns them in one statement, using `for update skip locked` so a second run gets a disjoint slice rather than blocking. This is the thing that makes the batch size a throughput decision instead of a gamble: the old shape selected unprocessed rows and wrote `processed_at` only after sending, which is safe exactly as long as a run finishes inside its own cron minute, and re-sends the whole batch to everyone the moment one doesn't. It is deliberately at-most-once (see "Notable design decisions"). The batch size is applied as `limit coalesce(p_limit, 200)`, not `limit p_limit` against a parameter default, for the reason in "Notable design decisions" below.

A scheduled Supabase Edge Function (`supabase/functions/send-push`, Deno, run every minute via pg_cron + pg_net) claims up to 200 events per run, computes recipients, and sends real push notifications over two channels depending on `push_subscriptions.platform`: `web-push` for browser/PWA subscribers (`endpoint`/`p256dh`/`auth_key`), and Firebase Cloud Messaging for the Capacitor native app (`fcm_token`) — one `push_subscriptions` row per device either way, `platform in ('web', 'android', 'ios')` with a same-table shape CHECK enforcing exactly one credential set per row (`20260722100000_native_push_tokens.sql`). FCM's HTTP v1 API needs a Google OAuth2 access token, not a static key, so `send-push` mints one itself from the `FCM_SERVICE_ACCOUNT_JSON_B64` secret via a self-signed JWT using Deno's native Web Crypto (`crypto.subtle`) — no JWT library needed for one call. Both channels send the same `{ title, body, url }` shape; a tap lands on the same target either way, via `sw.js`'s `notificationclick` handler on web or `components/pwa/NativePushNavigation.tsx`'s `notificationActionPerformed` listener natively. Dead subscriptions (404/410 from the push service, or `UNREGISTERED`/`NOT_FOUND` from FCM) are cleaned up automatically. `FCM_SERVICE_ACCOUNT_JSON_B64` is optional — a project that hasn't set up Firebase yet just skips native sends instead of failing the whole event. Stored base64-encoded rather than as raw JSON, and its parse is wrapped in try/catch even though it's top-level module code: a Google service account key is nothing but double quotes and embedded newlines, exactly what shell argument quoting mangles first when running `supabase secrets set`, and this bit the project for real once already — a malformed secret crashed the function's module initialization outright, taking down *all* push (web included) since a module that throws before `Deno.serve()` never serves a single request, not just the FCM path.

Inside a run the work is split into deciding and sending, and that split is the whole reason the throughput ceiling moved. `eventDeliveries()` resolves an event's recipients and copy into a list of `{ userId, content }` without sending anything, and those run over the claimed batch with a small concurrency (`EVENT_BUILD_CONCURRENCY`, database reads only). `deliverAll()` then looks up subscriptions for the entire batch in chunks of 100 user ids — chunked because supabase-js sends selects as GET, so an `in.(...)` list of UUIDs becomes URL text — and runs every resulting push through one pool of `SEND_CONCURRENCY` (20). The previous shape did a subscriptions query per recipient and awaited each send in turn inside two nested loops, so a 40-person event cost 40 round trips before the first notification left the building. A per-run promise cache (`lookup()`) memoizes the small repeated reads (`groupRow`, `marketRow`, `resolutionWindowLabel`), which matters because a batch is usually several events from the same group; it is reset per run so a warm isolate can't serve a stale group name after a rename. Both `groupRow` and `marketRow` return `null` instead of throwing when the row is gone — `delete_group()` is an immediate, unscheduled delete that cascades to `markets` and to `notification_events` itself, so a run can have already claimed an event a split second before the group behind it disappears. `buildContent()` resolves the group once at the top and bails out (returns `null`, one skipped push) on a miss, same as its existing `!event.market_id` check; before this it force-unwrapped the row and crashed with a bare `TypeError`. **Events is the wrong unit for capacity** — one `admin_broadcast` to a 40-person group is a single event and 40-odd sends — so `SEND_CONCURRENCY`, not `EVENT_BATCH_LIMIT`, is the knob that governs how fast a run drains.

**Ongoing experiment (added 2026-09-12, unresolved):** the `admin` client sends `Connection: close` on every request — a guess at fixing a climbing rate of PostgREST killing this function's calls with "Thread killed by timeout manager" (504s), on the theory that a warm isolate was reusing an HTTP connection PostgREST had already torn down. See the comment above the `admin` client in `index.ts` for the full reasoning and the log query to judge it by. Update this note with the outcome once there's enough data to keep or revert it — don't leave it in this provisional state.

Current copy (edit in `supabase/functions/send-push/index.ts`, then `supabase functions deploy send-push --no-verify-jwt`):

| Event | Body |
|---|---|
| Needs endorsement | A new market needs an endorser before it can open: "{title}" |
| Market opened | Betting's open on a new market: "{title}" |
| New system markets opened (NFL/CFB, 2+ created in one pipeline run) | New markets just opened in {group}. Take a look and get your bets in. |
| Market opened about you (subjects only) | A new market just opened about you. No spoilers, but you can watch the action. |
| Market closed | Betting just closed, odds are live: "{title}" |
| Resolution proposed | Someone proposed how "{title}" resolved. You have {resolution_window_hours} to challenge it. |
| Resolution challenged | The proposed resolution for "{title}" was challenged, cast your vote. |
| Resolved (subject) | A market about you just resolved, come see what it was: "{title}" |
| Resolved (a winning bettor) | You won {tokens} tokens! "{title}" resolved: {outcome}. |
| Resolved (a winner whose payout equals their stake) | You called "{title}" right: {outcome}. Nobody took the other side, so your {tokens} tokens come straight back. |
| Resolved (voided, had a stake) | "{title}" was voided, your {tokens} tokens were refunded. |
| Resolved (everyone else) | "{title}" resolved: {outcome}. See how it played out. |
| Season ended | Season {number} just wrapped up. Check the final standings and start the next one when you're ready. |
| Betting opened | Betting just opened. Be the first to start a market. |
| Impressive bet (to the bettor only) | You just pulled off the biggest underdog win in {group}'s history, {multiple}x on "{title}"! |
| Member joined (to the owner only) | @{nickname} just joined {group}. Say hi, or get a market going. |
| Assigned as moderator (to the new moderator only, public groups) | You're now a moderator of {group}. You can create markets and remove bad actors. |
| Market voided by owner | The owner voided "{title}" and refunded every stake. |
| Clarification requested (to the creator only) | @{nickname} isn't clear on how "{title}" resolves and is asking you to clarify. |
| Criteria updated | The resolution criteria for "{title}" just got clearer, take a look. |
| Weekend nudge | Weekend's nearly here. Anyone up to something worth betting on? |
| Market closing soon (lapsed bettors only) | Betting closes in 2 hours on "{title}". It's been a minute since your last one. |
| Group deletion canceled | False alarm, the owner canceled the deletion of {group}. |
| Group deletion scheduled (inactivity) | Nobody's started a new season in {group} for 30 days, so it'll be deleted for good in 5 days unless someone continues it. |
| Group deletion notice, 14 days out (any group, general inactivity) | Nobody's started a market or placed a bet in {group} for 90 days, so it'll be deleted for good in 14 days unless the owner cancels it. |
| Group deletion notice, 7 days out | {group} will be deleted for inactivity in 7 days unless the owner cancels it. |
| Group deletion notice, 1 day out | {group} will be deleted for inactivity tomorrow unless the owner cancels it. |
| Season betting opened | Betting just opened for the season. Time to start a market. |
| Awards updated | The Awards just shuffled. See who holds what now. |
| Admin broadcast | *(whatever the admin typed — `custom_title`/`custom_body`, the only event with author-supplied copy)* |

"Resolved" is the one event whose copy varies per recipient, not just per subject/non-subject: `send-push` computes each recipient's own settled winnings on that market (summed across any bets, in case of a hedge) and quotes it directly for a winner, falling back to a shared "here's what happened" copy for everyone else with a non-zero result cached by that shared key, so a large group's blast doesn't rebuild identical content once per recipient. It also sums each recipient's **stake**, because `payout == stake` is a meaningfully different outcome from a real win: it can only happen on a one-sided market (`total_pool == winning_pool`, so the parimutuel formula returns the stake exactly, dust included), where there were no losing stakes to split. Quoting that as "you won N tokens" overstates it, and framing it as winning your own bet back reads like a consolation prize when the call was in fact right, so that case gets its own copy. The reveal page's ledger makes the same distinction: "Won / N returned" instead of "+0 won".

**`market.outcome === 'void'` is checked before the `payout == stake` branch, not folded into it.** `refund_all_bets()` sets every bet's `payout` to its own `amount`, so a voided market's bettors hit the exact same `winnings === staked` condition a genuine one-sided win does — without the explicit check, `buildContent` would tell a bettor "You called it right" about a market that was never decided at all. The void branch (checked first, so the coincidence can never shadow it) says the stake was refunded instead, with a plain "resolved: voided" fallback for a recipient who never bet on it.

**`group_deletion_scheduled` (distinct from the still-live `_inactivity` variant above) is dead code, not a live event.** Now that an owner-initiated `delete_group` is immediate (see below), nothing emits it anymore — `send-push`'s `buildContent` still has a case for it, and the enum value still exists, but no function ever writes that event type into `notification_events`, so the handler can never actually run. Left in place rather than ripped out purely because it wasn't worth a bigger cleanup for dead code that costs nothing sitting idle; don't be misled into thinking a manual delete still pushes this.

**"Market opened about you" is the one deliberate crack in `is_market_visible()`'s otherwise-total pre-resolution wall.** Every other subject-facing surface (the market page, the group feed, every RPC) treats "subject + not yet resolved" as 404 — this event exists anyway because knowing *that* a market about you exists, with genuinely zero content, was judged worth breaking that wall for; nothing else was. `create_market()` (its direct-`open` path) and `sponsor_market()` both emit it alongside their existing `market_opened`, but only when the market actually has subjects, and `get_event_recipients` gives it the mirror image of every other market-scoped branch: subjects *only*, straight off `market_subjects` rather than `get_notification_recipients()` at all (every other branch does the opposite — excludes subjects by default). The push body never touches `market.title` — `buildContent`'s `market_opened_about_you` case is the one market-scoped switch arm that ignores the `market`/`group` values it's handed for exactly that reason. It deep-links to the same `/groups/{groupId}/markets/{marketId}` URL a `market_opened` push would use for anyone else; the market page itself decides what to render, not the push. `get_subject_market_pulse(_sides)` are two new SECURITY DEFINER functions, deliberately *not* built on `is_market_visible()` (that function's entire purpose is to say no here) — each does its own explicit check (real member of the group, an actual subject of *this* market, not yet `resolved`/`voided`) and hands back only: `status`/`market_type`/`closes_at`, total `bet_count`/`pool_amount`, and — for `yes_no`/`over_under` only — the odds split by side (`_sides` raises rather than returning a breakdown for `multiple_choice`, since option labels are exactly the kind of content this exists to hide). The market page tries `visible_markets` first as always; only when that comes back empty does it fall back to `get_subject_market_pulse`, rendering `SubjectMarketPulse` (no title, no description, no creator/subject list, no betting UI) instead of `notFoundIfEmpty`'s 404 if that succeeds too — anyone it's not actually meant for (wrong group, not a subject, market doesn't exist) still gets the same indistinguishable 404 as before, since the RPC raises `not_found` for all of those identically.
