Odds are shown even while the market is still `open`, unlike `get_closed_odds`' closed-only gate for everyone else — that gate exists to stop a bettor from being swayed by live odds while they can still act on them, which can't apply to a subject, since `is_market_visible()` already blocks `place_bet` for them same as everything else.

Reactions (`react_to_market`) are deliberately push-free — a resolved market's reveal ticket is a low-stakes, browse-when-you-feel-like-it surface, not something worth interrupting anyone for.

### Notification preferences

`users.notifications_enabled` is still the master kill switch, but it is no longer the only control. Preferences are split into the three different *reasons* a push arrives, not into a taxonomy of event types:

| Category | Where it's stored | What it covers |
|---|---|---|
| general (per group) | `memberships.notify_group` + `notify_markets` / `notify_results` / `notify_admin` | something happened in a group you're in |
| nudges | `users.notify_nudges` | nothing happened, and that's the point |
| promos | `users.notify_promos` | the app itself is talking to you (`admin_broadcast`, the only channel that exists to market at someone) |

**The page is one master switch over a per-group accordion.** `NotificationPreferences`'s first card, "All notifications", *is* this device's push subscription (`usePushSubscription`'s subscribe/unsubscribe, which also writes `users.notifications_enabled`) — it is not a fourth category. It reads as a master because it is one: nothing below it can arrive if it's off. The platform cases push can't serve (unsupported browser, iOS before install, permission already refused) replace that row's subtitle and disable its switch rather than swapping in a differently-shaped card, so the page never changes structure based on the device it's opened on. Below it, each group is one row carrying its master `notify_group` switch plus a one-line summary of which categories are on ("Markets and results on", "Results only", "Muted"); tapping the row expands the three category switches. The sub-switches stay mounted but dimmed and inert when the group is muted, because they're the answer to "what would I get back if I turned this on again" — unmounting them makes the master look like it deleted them.

**The general category lives on `memberships`, not in a new preferences table.** Identity in this app is already per-group (`memberships.nickname`), a membership row already exists for exactly the (user, group) pairs that can receive a group notification, and `get_event_recipients` already joins that table in every branch. A separate table would have needed its own RLS, its own lifecycle on join/leave/rejoin, and a left join with a default at every read.

**Nudges and promos are global rather than per-group on purpose.** "Stop pestering me" and "no marketing" are feelings about the app, not about one friend group, and making them per-group would mean re-answering them for every group you join. `_prefs_allow` still gates nudges on the per-group `notify_group` switch (a nudge is about one specific group, so muting that group mutes its nudges), while promos deliberately ignore it (they aren't really about the group they happen to be addressed through).

`_notification_category` maps every event type to one of these, and an **unmapped type falls through to `other`, which `_prefs_allow` lets through**. That's the deliberate failure mode: a new event type someone forgot to categorize gets delivered rather than silently dropped.

**`set_notifications_enabled()` exists because the old code path silently did nothing.** `setNotificationsEnabled()` used to do a plain `supabase.from('users').update(...)` from a Server Action, but `users` has select and insert policies and deliberately no update policy — an RLS-filtered UPDATE matches zero rows and reports success rather than erroring, so the master toggle had been a no-op. Routing it through a SECURITY DEFINER function is both the fix and what this codebase's "all mutation goes through SECURITY DEFINER functions" rule required in the first place. Worth remembering as a general trap: **a blocked UPDATE through PostgREST is indistinguishable from a successful one that matched nothing.**

### Marketing email consent

`users.marketing_email_opt_in` (`20260830210000_marketing_email_opt_in.sql`) is a separate consent from `notify_promos` above — that one gates a *push*, this one is an opt-in checkbox on the signup form (`SignUpForm`, unchecked by default, deliberately its own checkbox rather than folded into the required "I agree to the Terms/Privacy policy" one, since agreeing to the terms is mandatory and hearing from us by email isn't) for a channel nothing in this app actually sends through yet. It exists ahead of any real email-sending integration so the consent record exists before it's ever needed, not retrofitted once it is. `marketing_email_opt_in_at` stamps whenever the choice last changed.

The value travels from the signup form to wherever the profile row actually gets created via Supabase auth's `user_metadata` (`options.data` on `supabase.auth.signUp()`), not a plain function argument or a hidden form field — `ensureProfileRow()` (`lib/actions/auth.ts`) is called from three different completion paths (an immediate session, the 6-digit code screen, and the emailed confirmation link in `app/auth/confirm/route.ts`, which is a bare `GET` with no form data at all), and `user_metadata` is the one thing that survives all three without having to be threaded through `ConfirmEmailForm` as a second hidden field alongside `email`/`next`. `update_marketing_email_opt_in()` is the later off-ramp, surfaced as a "By email" toggle on `/profile/notifications` next to "News from Barbets" (the push equivalent), since a consent captured once at signup with no way to revisit it is a dead end.

### Policy reapproval

`lib/legal.ts`'s `CURRENT_POLICY_VERSION` and `users.accepted_policy_version`/`accepted_policy_at` (`20260830220000_policy_reapproval.sql`) exist so a material change to the Terms of use or Privacy policy can require existing users to explicitly re-agree, rather than relying solely on the "continued use of Barbets after a change means you accept it" clause both `app/terms/page.tsx` and `app/privacy/page.tsx` already carry (that clause is still there, but now describes the fallback for a change too small to bother bumping the version for). One shared version for both documents, not one each, since the signup checkbox already treats "Terms of use and Privacy policy" as a single agreement.

**To trigger a reapproval prompt: bump `CURRENT_POLICY_VERSION` to a new value (any string works; a date is the convention) in the same change that edits the policy pages.** That's the entire mechanism — no migration needed for a version bump, since the version lives in application code and the database only ever stores what a given user last agreed to, compared against whatever the app currently says is current.

`app/(app)/layout.tsx` reads the signed-in user's `accepted_policy_version` on every request (one extra `select` alongside the `touch_last_active()` call already there) and renders `PolicyReapprovalGate` when it doesn't match — a full-screen, undismissable block, deliberately (a suggestion is not a gate), with a checkbox mirroring the signup one and a "Continue" button that calls `acceptCurrentPolicy()` (`lib/actions/legal.ts`) and then `router.refresh()`, so the parent layout is what decides to stop rendering it, off the same read that decided to start. `acceptCurrentPolicy()` always stamps the server's own `CURRENT_POLICY_VERSION` rather than trusting a client-supplied version string, so a stale client can't accept-and-dismiss against a version that's no longer current. Scoped to `app/(app)/layout.tsx` only (the signed-in consumer app), not `/admin` or the signed-out marketing pages — a brand-new signup is stamped at today's version by `ensureProfileRow()` the moment their account is created, so they never see this gate until a future bump.

**Existing accounts were backfilled to the version live at the time this shipped** (`2026-08-27`, hardcoded as a literal in the migration, matching `CURRENT_POLICY_VERSION`'s value at that moment), so nobody was retroactively put through the new gate for policies nobody actually rewrote. Only a version bump *after* that point ever surfaces the prompt.

## Platform admin, feedback, and access gates

**This section describes `/admin` as it exists in this repo today, which is mid-migration to its own site.** `admin.mybarbets.com` (a separate repo, `barbets-admin` — see "Domains and the mybarbets.com split" under Deployment) now hosts the same broadcast/public-group/moderator/pipeline/QR-totals tooling plus a new `/growth` growth-analytics dashboard, reading and writing through the identical RPCs below. `app/(app)/admin/page.tsx` and `components/admin/*` here are kept until that site is confirmed working end to end, at which point they get deleted in a follow-up change — if you're reading this after that cleanup landed, this whole subsection (Postgres functions/RLS unaffected) should be trimmed to a pointer at `barbets-admin`.

**`app_admins` is the app's second authority model, and the only one that isn't per-group.** Everywhere else in this codebase, "can you do this?" resolves to `groups.owner_id` or to membership in a specific group; `app_admins` (one column, `user_id`) is platform-wide, and `is_platform_admin(p_user_id default auth.uid())` is the single choke point every admin-gated function and page calls first. The table is zero-policy (see the data model above) — nothing reads it directly, only that function does. `/admin` follows the same 404-not-403 rule as everything else: a non-admin doesn't get "you don't have permission," they get `notFound()`, so the page's existence isn't even confirmed.

The admin surface is deliberately small: `get_platform_admin_stats()` (active groups / total markets / total users), `list_groups_for_admin()` / `list_group_members_for_admin()` (the broadcast form's pickers), `send_admin_broadcast(p_group_id, p_title, p_body, p_target_user_id default null)`, and (see "Public groups" below) `create_public_group()` / `assign_group_moderator()` / `list_group_moderator_candidates()`. All go through SECURITY DEFINER functions rather than the service-role client (`lib/supabase/admin.ts`), which stays reserved for the two things genuinely impossible in SQL: bypassing Storage on the `resolution-proofs` bucket, and deleting an `auth.users` row. A platform-wide aggregate count is ordinary data access that just needs gating, which is exactly what SECURITY DEFINER is for.

**The group/user analytics surface is a second, larger tier of admin RPCs**, all `admin_*`-named, all `is_platform_admin()`-gated the same way, backing `barbets-admin`'s `/groups` and `/users` pages (GRP-STATE, GRP-DRILL, USR-SEG, CHURN) and a new section on its existing `/growth` page (the frequency-of-use bundle: photo proof usage, share clicks, season length choice, settings-change frequency) — see the "Data model" section above for the full list, the underlying enums, and the reasoning behind each one's thresholds. `barbets-admin` still has no `ARCHITECTURE.md` of its own as of this change, so this doc remains the sole source of truth for all of it, same as for the rest of the admin surface described in this section.

**`admin_broadcast` is the one notification event with author-supplied copy.** It exists to test marketing/ad creative on real devices, and reuses the existing `notification_events` → `send-push` pipeline rather than opening a second delivery path, because the VAPID/FCM secrets are Edge-Function-only and unreachable from Next.js. It carries `custom_title`/`custom_body` (bypassing `_emit_notification_event()`, whose signature has no room for them and which 8 other call sites depend on) plus `target_user_id`, the only event type that has one. In `get_event_recipients` it gets its own branch rather than joining the shared group-scoped list, for two reasons: it can address one specific member instead of the whole group, and when it's targeted it deliberately **skips the usual actor exclusion** — an admin targeting themselves to check their own device should actually receive it, unlike an accidental self-broadcast.

**Feedback is stored in Postgres and delivered to Slack, in that order.** `submit_feedback()` (the only way into the zero-policy `feedback` table) writes the durable row — message, `category` (`bug`/`idea`/`general`), `wants_followup`, `page_url`, and a `group_id` that `lib/actions/feedback.ts` extracts from the referring URL when the submitter happened to be on a group page. Only *then* does the action post a Slack Block Kit card via `SLACK_FEEDBACK_WEBHOOK_URL`, best-effort inside a try/catch: the feedback is already durably saved by that point, so a Slack outage must never fail the submission. This posts directly rather than routing through `notification_events`, because unlike VAPID/FCM there's no secret here that Next.js can't hold.

**Production errors are tracked by posting them to Slack, with no third-party service.** `lib/errorReporter.ts`'s `reportError()` is the one place an unexpected throw becomes a Block Kit card on `SLACK_ERROR_WEBHOOK_URL` (a second Incoming Webhook on the same Slack app the feedback form already uses, pointed at a different channel). Three feeds reach it:

- **`instrumentation.ts`'s `onRequestError`** — Next.js's own hook, called for every error thrown out of server rendering, a Server Action, a route handler, or the proxy, including the ones the user only ever sees as a redacted digest. One hook instead of a try/catch in each of the ~40 places that could throw.
- **`app/global-error.tsx`** (new, and also the app's first global error boundary at all) — a client render crash never reaches the server on its own, and in an installed PWA that is most of what a user experiences as "the app broke". It posts back through the `reportClientError()` Server Action (`lib/actions/errorReport.ts`). It **skips reporting when `error.digest` is set**, since a digest means the error came from the server and `onRequestError` already sent it with a real un-minified stack.
- **`supabase/functions/send-push`** — its own ~30-line copy of the same idea (an Edge Function shares no code with the app bundle, and this file already prefers a few lines over a dependency). It runs on a cron with nobody reading its logs, so a broken push run used to be silent in both senses. Set the same webhook as a Supabase secret to turn it on: `npx supabase secrets set SLACK_ERROR_WEBHOOK_URL=...`. It also reports **`expire_stale()`'s** failures, via `claim_unreported_sweep_failures()` after the pushes go out: nothing in Postgres can post to Slack, and this function already runs every minute and already holds the webhook. Claiming marks rows reported in the same statement that returns them, so a row failing every minute produces one card rather than 1,440 a day. `_record_sweep_failure` then re-arms the flag on the 10th and 100th attempt and every 1000th after that (roughly 10 minutes, 1.5 hours, then every 17 hours at one attempt a minute), so a genuinely stuck row nags occasionally instead of resting entirely on someone having caught the first card. Counted in attempts rather than elapsed time, since attempts is what measures how stuck a row actually is: something that fails once and then succeeds never escalates. It deliberately doesn't claim when no webhook is configured — marking a failure reported with nowhere to report it would throw away the only signal.

**Every card carries plain-English triage, not just a stack trace.** A stack answers "what threw" but not the two things you need when the phone buzzes: is this bad, and what do I do now. `TRIAGE` in `lib/errorReporter.ts` matches nine failure shapes this stack actually produces (duplicate Postgres function overload, missing table/column, empty-value dereference, renamed export, hydration mismatch, network blip, statement timeout, session/JWT, and a still-useful fallback) and attaches a *What's happening* and a *What to do* section, each with one concrete first move: `npx supabase db push` for the migration-didn't-ship case, "check status.supabase.com before changing code" for a network blip, "paste this card into Claude Code" otherwise. A footer restates on every card that no balance can be affected, since every money change is a single transactional Postgres function call. `send-push` carries its own four-case version tuned to what breaks there (including the base64 FCM secret, which has bitten this project for real). When adding a case, match on something specific enough not to swallow unrelated errors and keep the fix to one action.

**Both reporters check the Slack response.** A malformed Block Kit payload comes back as a 400 with a body naming the offending block, *not* as a failed request — so an unchecked `fetch` would mean an empty channel and no trace of why. Non-2xx is logged with its status and body. Silent silence is the one failure mode error reporting cannot afford.

Why not Sentry or similar: the deliverable is "a Slack message when something breaks," and that is an HTTP POST to a URL this app already knows how to make. A hosted tracker would add a runtime dependency, a build step, a vendor account, and a paid plan for the Slack integration itself, to deliver the same message to the same channel. What a tracker really sells is grouping, rate limiting, and retention — the first two are cheap here (a fingerprint of `source|route|name|message|first stack frame`, capped at 3 reports per fingerprint and 30 overall per 10-minute window, per instance) and the third is what the Slack channel is. If the volume ever outgrows a channel, `reportError()` is the single seam to swap.

Two filters keep the channel honest, and both matter more here than in a typical app: **Next's control-flow throws** (`NEXT_NOT_FOUND`, `NEXT_REDIRECT`, ...) are skipped, because the 404-never-403 rule means `notFound()` is thrown on every hidden-market read and is completely routine; and **an `ActionError` with a known business-rule code** (`not_found`/`forbidden`/`invalid_operation`/`insufficient_balance`) is skipped, because those are the database saying no on purpose. Only an `unknown` code is a real fault. The check is duck-typed on `.code` rather than `instanceof ActionError`, since `instrumentation.ts` is its own module graph. Note that Server Actions barely feature here by design: they *return* business-rule errors rather than throwing them, so anything that does throw out of one is genuinely unexpected. Reporting is gated to `VERCEL_ENV === 'production'` (falling back to `NODE_ENV` when there's no `VERCEL_ENV`), because preview deploys also run with `NODE_ENV=production` and would otherwise page you for every branch. `console.error` still fires in every environment regardless — the console is the record that always exists, Slack is the one that pages you.

**"Waiting on you" tasks (`lib/tasks.ts`) partially walk back the Inbox deletion.** `getGroupTasks()` returns the two market states genuinely blocked on one specific viewer — endorse a `pending_sponsor` market they didn't create, vote on a `disputed` market where they're neither a hidden subject nor already a voter — mirroring `sponsor_market`/`cast_vote`'s own eligibility exactly, so the UI never offers a task the RPC would reject. It feeds three surfaces: the group hub's `WaitingOnYouCard` (full list), the all-groups page's per-row count, and a red dot on BottomNav's Home tab (batched via `getGroupTaskCounts()`). This is the count and list only, **not** a re-homed Inbox page — see the BottomNav design-decision note below for what was deleted and why this much came back. `WaitingOnYouCard`'s dismissal is keyed to a *signature* of the current task set rather than a plain seen/unseen flag, so a new task automatically un-dismisses the card instead of it staying hidden forever after one dismiss.

**The beta gate is a single flag, currently off.** `lib/betaGate.ts` exports `BETA_GATE_ENABLED` (`false` today), a shared code, and a cookie name; when on, `proxy.ts` bounces an uncookied `/login` to `/under-construction`, and `checkBetaCode()` sets a 30-day httpOnly cookie on a correct code. It's built to be deleted — turning the flag off is enough, and nothing else in the app depends on it. Its `safeNext()` only ever redirects to a relative in-app path, never an absolute URL from form input; `app/auth/confirm/route.ts` applies the identical guard to its own `next` param.

**Both recovery and signup confirmation emails use `verifyOtp`, not PKCE code exchange, and both had to be pointed at `/auth/confirm` by hand.** Supabase's default `{{ .ConfirmationURL }}` link verifies against Supabase's own hosted `/auth/v1/verify` endpoint and redirects back with the session in a URL fragment, fine for a client-only SPA but invisible to this app's server-rendered pages (fragments never reach the server) — clicking it landed a freshly confirmed signup right back on the signed-out splash with nothing to show for it, indistinguishable from the link having failed. Both the Reset Password and Confirm signup templates are customized in the Supabase dashboard to link to `/auth/confirm` with `token_hash`/`type`/`next` instead; that route calls `verifyOtp` server-side, which sets the session as a cookie the next request actually sees, then redirects to `next` (`/reset-password` for recovery, `/groups` for signup — landing signed in, inside the app, is itself the confirmation that it worked). Signup additionally needs `ensureProfileRow()` here (recovery's user already has one, so it's a harmless no-op there), and a failed/expired link needs to land somewhere sensible for *that* flow — recovery back on `/forgot-password`, signup back on `/login?mode=signup` — rather than always the recovery page regardless of which one failed. If either email's link ever stops working, check its template's `href` first — this flow depends on it not being reverted to the default. **This has happened for real**: as of 2026-08-27 the Reset Password template had reverted to plain `{{ .ConfirmationURL }}` (Confirm signup was unaffected), so every recovery link hit Supabase's own verify endpoint, redirected with the session in a fragment `/reset-password` can't see, and the page fell into its no-session branch and showed "This link is invalid or has expired" for a token that had actually just been consumed successfully — a dashboard-only setting silently drifting from what this doc assumed, with no diff or CI check to catch it. Re-pointed via the Supabase management API's `PATCH /v1/projects/{ref}/config/auth` (`mailer_templates_recovery_content`), which is worth knowing about as a faster path than the dashboard UI for verifying or fixing template drift like this again.

## Public groups

A small, deliberate exception to "every group is a private table you're invited to." A handful of always-on, staff-owned groups exist so a brand-new user can try Barbets — browse a directory, join instantly, place a bet — before they've talked a single friend into creating a real group. Phase 1 is the infrastructure and manual moderation described first below; Phase 2, the auto-generated NFL/CFB market pipeline, is its own subsection near the end of this one.

**Schema.** `groups.is_public` (boolean) and `groups.category` (`'generic'` or `'campus'`, null for an ordinary private group) — a CHECK constraint ties the two together so a private group can never carry a category and a public one can never lack one. `group_settings.awards_enabled` (default `true`) is forced to `false` for every public group — see below. `prize_text`/`punishment_text` are forced `null` there too, same reasoning as `join_message`: a public group's settings are a fixed rule set, not a place for owner flavor text.

**A third authority tier, additive to the existing two.** Ownership is still entirely `groups.owner_id` (a public group's owner is literally whichever platform admin created it, via `create_public_group()` — not a separate system user) and platform authority is still entirely `app_admins`/`is_platform_admin()` (see "Platform admin" above). What's new is `memberships.role` (`'member'` or `'moderator'`, default `'member'`) — the first multi-person-per-group authority in this codebase. Deliberately not a third enum value alongside an owner marker: duplicating "who owns this" into a role column would just be two places that could drift, so `_is_group_mod_or_owner(p_group_id, p_user_id)` is the one choke point every mod-gated function calls, true for the group's owner (unchanged) or an active/dormant member whose `role = 'moderator'`. A mod's authority now covers everything a public group needs day to day — hand-create a market, void a bad auto-generated one, ban a bad actor, **and edit settings** (`update_group_settings()`'s owner-only check gained an `is_public` branch: `_is_group_mod_or_owner()` instead of a bare `owner_id` match, with the caller's own membership requirement dropped too when they're the actual owner — see below for why that specifically matters). True owner-only actions (delete the group, transfer ownership) stay owner-only. `assign_group_moderator()` (platform-admin-only, scoped to public groups — a private group's single owner already has full authority on their own) is how a moderator gets flipped on/off, from the admin console's "Public groups & moderators" modal; `forfeit_moderator()` is the mirror self-service action — a moderator can step back to a regular member without leaving the group entirely, no authorization beyond "you currently hold the role" since it only ever lowers the caller's own privilege. Demoting a group's *last* moderator via `assign_group_moderator()` is rejected outright (`invalid_operation: this group has no other moderator, assign a replacement first`) rather than silently leaving a public group with nobody but the owner running it day to day.

**All public groups share a fixed rule set, not per-group preferences** — enforced twice over: `update_group_settings()` coerces the relevant `group_settings` columns server-side regardless of what's submitted (`if v_group.is_public then p_seasons_enabled := false; ...`), and `create_public_group()` sets every one of them explicitly at creation rather than relying on column defaults, so a public group can never momentarily exist without the rules that define it. A one-time backfill (`20260825100000`) corrected the handful of public groups created before this coercion existed.

- **Only mods (or the owner) can hand-create a market.** `create_market()` checks `_is_group_mod_or_owner()` immediately after its membership check and raises `forbidden` otherwise. The "+" button's create sheet (`BottomNav`) and the `/markets/new` route both pre-check the same thing (`app/(app)/layout.tsx` computes a `not_moderator` blocked reason per group; the route itself 404s a non-mod the same way `settings/edit` 404s a non-owner) so a regular public-group member never even reaches a form that would just reject them — the Postgres check is still what's actually load-bearing.
- **No markets about a specific person.** Same function: a public group's `create_market()` call rejects any `@mention` option and any non-empty `p_subject_user_ids`, before any of the subject-cap math that only matters for a real subject. `CreateMarketForm` takes an `isPublic` prop that drops the "About someone?" card and the @mention hint entirely, rather than offering something that would only come back as an error.
- **No awards.** `_recompute_group_titles()` and `_upsert_risk_taker()` (the live-updating Risk Taker title) both early-return when `awards_enabled` is off, so a public group's `group_titles` table never populates at all rather than populating and having the UI choose not to render it. The Awards page shows a plain "not available in this group" state instead of `AwardsRail`/`CustomAwardsSection`, and the Leaderboard page's Awards row is hidden the same way.
- **No seasons, no hedging, no endorsement.** `seasons_enabled`/`allow_hedged_bets`/`require_endorsement` are all forced `false` — a public group runs one continuous board forever, a member can back more than one side without asking permission being a non-issue since there's no "asking permission" setting to turn off, and `create_market()` puts a new market straight into `open` (never `pending_sponsor`), so `sponsor_market()` is simply never reachable there.
- **Always accepting members, betting always on.** `accepting_members`/`betting_enabled` are forced `true` — there's no "owner sets things up first" period and no invite-only pause; the directory listing itself is the only gate.
- **A universal loss (nobody calls it right) pools 100% into the group's other open markets.** `distribute_payout` is forced `true` and `creator_payout_pct` forced `0` — `_finalize_market_core()`'s existing zero-winning-pool branch already does exactly "the whole remaining pool tops up other open markets, or `pending_bonus_pool` if there are none" once the creator's cut is zero, so this is a pure settings-coercion change with no new money-path code.
- **Results are instant — no challenge window.** `propose_resolution()` calls `finalize_market()` in the very same transaction for a public group's market (`if v_is_public then perform finalize_market(p_market_id); end if;`), so the market never sits in `proposed` long enough for anyone to challenge it. `_finalize_market_core()`'s own "challenge window still open" check is skipped for a public group for exactly this reason — without that, the immediate call would raise on its own freshly-inserted proposal (`proposed_at` is `now()`, never past any window). `challenge_resolution()`/`cast_vote()` need no code change: their own status checks (`market has no pending proposal` / `not open for voting`) already reject a market that's already `resolved`/`voided`, which is what a public-group market always is by the time either could be called. The Settings read view shows a plain "Instant" results row instead of the challenge-window slider. **Only mods (or the owner) can propose a resolution** (`propose_resolution()`'s `is_public` branch, `20260827100000`) — since it instantly finalizes with real money moving, letting any directory-joined stranger resolve was a much bigger hole than in a private group of friends. `ProposeResolutionCard` takes a `canResolve` prop computed the same way the "+" create button already is, so a regular member never sees a button that would only come back `forbidden`.
- **Simplified leaderboard.** Already free: the leaderboard page renders nothing but a net-tokens ranking regardless of group type, and every richer stat lives off the titles/custom-awards system, which is already off.
- **Nickname content filtering.** `_nickname_contains_blocked_word()` is a plain substring check against a fixed profanity/slur list, applied wherever a public-group nickname gets set: `join_public_group()` (a new join, or a `left` member rejoining) and `update_nickname()` (gated on the membership's group being public — a private group's owner is trusted to self-police). Nicknames are a single token with no spaces, so there's no word boundary to match against the way a sentence-level filter would; this stops the overwhelming majority of casual attempts, not a determined bypass, which is the realistic bar for a v1.
- **No profile pictures, for anyone, anywhere in a public group — not even an initials placeholder.** A directory-joined roster is strangers, not an invited friend group — an uploaded photo carries none of the same trust and nobody moderates it, and even the empty avatar circle still reads as "a person's picture goes here." This is a display-layer rule, not an account-layer one: `users.avatar_updated_at`/`avatar_preset_key` are untouched, and a member's photo still shows on their own `/profile`/`/profile/account` and in any private group they're also in. Two things happen together everywhere a public group's roster is rendered: the avatar fields handed to `UserAvatar` are nulled (harmless on its own, `UserAvatar` already falls back to initials for anyone with no photo), **and the `<UserAvatar>` element itself is skipped entirely** — an explicit `isPublicGroup`/`isPublic` boolean threaded through the data shape (`MemberProfileData`, `HeadToHeadData`) rather than inferred from both avatar fields being null, since that's also what an ordinary member with no photo looks like and would otherwise hide the *real* initials fallback a private-group member is supposed to get. Call sites: `getMemberProfileData()` (`lib/memberProfile.ts`), `loadHeadToHead()` (`lib/actions/memberProfile.ts`) and its full-page fallback (`members/[membershipId]/vs/[otherMembershipId]/page.tsx`), the leaderboard page (hero and every standings row), and the profile page's own `ShareRecordCard` hero when the *selected* group is public.
- **No `member_joined` push.** A private group's join is a rare, personal event worth pinging the owner about; a public group's is a directory browse-and-join that can happen constantly, with no single "the mods" recipient list that scales with it the way one owner does. `join_public_group()` simply never emits the event — `member_joined` is unaffected everywhere else.

**Settings are stripped down to almost nothing for a public group** — `EditSettingsForm`/`GroupPlaysCard`/the settings page all take an `isPublic` prop that drops every row for a rule that's now fixed (endorsement, hedging, accepting-members, betting, universal-loss split, challenge window, season length, join message) and drops the invite-code section entirely (nothing needs one — see below). **Token allocation and time zone are the only settings left, editable or otherwise shown.** `/groups/[groupId]/settings/edit`'s access gate reflects the same authority change as `update_group_settings()` itself: a private group is still owner-only, a public group also admits its moderators.

**The settings page header no longer carries a "you're the owner"/"member" pill** — the space it sat in is just the page's own "Settings" title now (previously conditional on role, now always the same word regardless of who's looking), and ownership/mod status is folded lightly into the existing member-count caption line instead (`"14 members · Owner"` / `"· Moderator"`) rather than called out with its own chip. Applies to both public and private groups.

**A public group's roster doesn't get browsed, it gets searched.** A directory-joined group can run into the hundreds of members, so the settings page's full-roster list (every private group's normal view) is replaced by `MemberSearchBan` — a nickname search box that renders nothing until there's a query, capped at 20 results. Its ban action is `remove_member()`, extended to accept a public group's moderators as callers, not just the owner (`if v_group.is_public then ... _is_group_mod_or_owner() ... else ... owner-only ...`) — the same "quick safety valve" mods already have for voiding a bad auto-generated market. A private group's `remove_member()` is unchanged, still owner-only.

**Ownership transfer stays inside the moderation team.** `transfer_ownership()` requires the new owner to already hold `role = 'moderator'` on a public group (`invalid_operation: pick an existing moderator... assign one first if there isn't one yet`) — this is what makes handing off resolve cleanly either way: if another mod exists, the owner transfers straight to them and stays on as a regular moderator; if they're the only mod, the transfer is rejected until they promote someone else via `assignGroupModerator()` first. `TransferOwnershipSheet`'s member picker is pre-filtered to moderators-only for a public group, and its copy points at the actual fix when that list is empty.

**Discovery directory + instant join.** `list_public_groups()` is a deliberately open read — the one group listing in this app not gated by `_caller_is_active_group_member()` — returning id/name/avatar/category/member-count, plus (as of `20260830280000`) `open_market_count` and one `featured_market_*` (id/title/bet-count, chosen soonest-closing-first) for every `is_public = true` group not scheduled for deletion. The featured market never carries a price or pool split — this app never reveals odds on a market that's still open (see "Sealed odds" below), and a directory card someone hasn't even joined yet is not the exception; the bet count is fine to expose the same way "N bets placed" already is on every open `MarketCard`/`MarketRow`. Widening the return shape needed the explicit `DROP FUNCTION` this project's function-change rule calls for (`CREATE OR REPLACE` can't change a function's return type). `join_public_group(p_group_id, p_nickname)` mirrors `join_group()`'s status/nickname/dormant/intermission handling exactly, just keyed by group id instead of an invite code; no rate limiting applies (nothing to guess), and a private group's id raises the same `not_found` a bad invite code would rather than becoming a second door into it.

`/groups/discover` (`DiscoverGroupCard`) is the browse UI. The groups hub also surfaces it directly: a zero-group user sees a full "Open to anyone" section above the invite-code card (up to two group cards, each with its featured market); everyone else sees a single collapsed shelf row instead, the same visual weight as the "Between seasons" row below it. Both the hub surface and the rebuilt `/groups/discover` are scoped to `category = 'generic'` (today: NFL and CFB) via `isHomeSurfacePublicGroup()` (`lib/publicGroups.ts`) — a `'campus'` group, if one ever exists, is reachable only through the "Request this group" form, not this browsing surface. Neither surface renders a per-member avatar for any public group, stacked or otherwise — that would reopen the "no profile pictures in a public group" rule above; a plain playing-count stands in where a design might otherwise reach for a face pile. There's no invite-code section anywhere in a public group's settings — nobody needs one.

**Admin tooling** (`lib/actions/admin.ts`, `/admin`). `createPublicGroup()` → `create_public_group()`: same validation shape as `create_group()` (name/seed-amount/nickname bounds), forces `is_public`/`category`/every hard-rule flag at creation. The creating admin no longer has to join their own group to create it — `p_nickname` is optional, and passing one is the explicit "I'll moderate this too" opt-in (a seeded, active, `role = 'moderator'` membership alongside the owner authority they already have via `groups.owner_id` either way); leaving it out means they administer purely from `/admin`, no membership at all. `p_moderator_emails` (`text[]`) adds each address that matches an existing Barbets account (looked up against `auth.users`, which a `SECURITY DEFINER` function owned by a privileged role can read directly) as an active, seeded, `role = 'moderator'` member immediately — no invite, no join step — with a nickname auto-derived from the email's local part (sanitized, deduped against the group, blocklist-checked, falling back to a generic `mod` base) since there's nobody at a join screen to type one in. Each gets `assigned_group_moderator`, a new single-recipient event (`actor_id` repurposed as "the one to notify," same convention `impressive_bet` established) pushing "You're now a moderator of {group}." An email matching no account is skipped and returned in `unresolved_emails` (the function's return type is a flattened `groups` row plus that one array column, not a bare `groups` row) so the admin console can show which addresses didn't take. Each public group is a single tappable row in the "Public groups & moderators" card; tapping opens `ManageModeratorsModal` (total members, category capitalized, the moderator list with a "Remove moderator" button per moderator, and a search-to-add box for everyone else that clears itself the moment a promotion succeeds, so the modal doesn't keep re-showing someone who just left the search results) rather than an inline expanding panel — nothing lists every member up front, same `MemberSearchBan` pattern. "Remove moderator" calls `assignGroupModerator(..., false)`, which `assign_group_moderator()` rejects server-side (`invalid_operation: this group has no other moderator, assign a replacement first`) if it's the group's last one.

**Auto-generated NFL/CFB markets (Phase 2).** Originally a single "Sports" group continuously polling The Odds API for MLB/NBA/NFL moneylines — it broke often (Odds API credit exhaustion, stuck-market pileups, a widening migration that silently dropped a flag — all still documented below since the lessons outlast the shape that produced them) and didn't draw many bets. `20260906100000` onward reworked it into a smaller, admin-curated weekly ritual instead of a continuously-polling firehose.

A second pipeline, an always-on "Weather" group creating automated markets from `api.weather.gov` forecasts, ran alongside Sports for a while and shared most of this infrastructure (`pipeline_settings`, `_create_system_market()`/`_resolve_system_market()`, `PipelineGroupFeed`, the pruning and notification-volume rules below). It's gone now — the group was deleted directly (2026-09-12) and `20260912091138_retire_weather_pipeline.sql` unscheduled its cron jobs, dropped its `pipeline_settings` row, and stripped the name-based special cases it had left in `_prune_resolved_system_markets()`, `get_member_stats()`, `list_pipeline_health()`, and `expire_stale()`. Nothing below describes it going forward; if it ever comes back it'd be a fresh build, not a revival of the old shape.

**NFL and CFB: one admin-picked marquee game a week, not a continuous moneyline board.** The original "Sports" group is renamed to **NFL** (`20260906100000` — a plain `update`, keeping its members/balances/history intact rather than starting from zero); **CFB** is a genuinely new public group, created live from the admin console's "New public group" form the same way any other public group is (`create_public_group()` needs a live `auth.uid()`, which is why the *original* Sports seed had to bypass it in a migration — CFB has no such constraint, since an admin is actually clicking the button). NFL is still seeded once via that original migration; see its own comment for the `auth.uid()`-in-a-migration workaround.

The weekly cycle, end to end:

- **Monday morning — `sports-weekly-prepare`** polls The Odds API's free `/events` endpoint (fixture data, no odds — this app runs its own pool and has never needed anyone else's lines, the same reasoning the original Sports pipeline used) for `americanfootball_nfl` and `americanfootball_ncaaf`, for each league filters to games starting at least 24 hours out (so nothing from the week that just finished slips in) and at most 8 days out (comfortably past next Monday night, the far edge of a full NFL week), and inserts one `game_of_week_picks` row per league keyed by `week_key` — the nearest Tuesday on or after "now," the day the market actually goes out. Idempotent by construction: a rerun in the same week is a no-op for any league that already has a row, whatever its status, so a retry can never clobber an admin's already-made pick or reshuffle the candidate list under them. Only when a row is newly created does it ping `SLACK_GAME_PICK_WEBHOOK_URL` (same fire-and-forget Incoming Webhook pattern as `lib/errorReporter.ts`/`lib/actions/feedback.ts`'s `postToSlack` — a new webhook, not a reuse of either of those, since this is a different audience and purpose) with each league's candidate count and a link to `/admin/game-of-the-week`.
- **The admin picks** from `/admin/game-of-the-week` (`GameOfWeekPicker`), a scrollable, kickoff-time-sorted candidate list per league with a "Pick" action per row — `pickGameOfWeek()` → `pick_game_of_week()`, which validates the chosen event id is actually one of that row's own candidates and is re-callable ("Change pick") any time before Tuesday's publish locks it. The last candidate in each league's list (latest kickoff) carries a small "auto-pick fallback" hint, naming exactly what happens if nobody picks in time rather than leaving that as a surprise.
- **Tuesday morning — `sports-weekly-publish`** turns each league's row into the real market: the admin's own pick if one was made, or — so a group never goes dark on an admin who didn't get to it — the candidate with the latest kickoff as a sensible, odds-free default (the closest this pipeline gets to "the marquee game" without paying for real odds, which this app has never needed elsewhere either). **The market is `multiple_choice` with the two team names as its options, "{home} vs. {away}" as the title — not `yes_no`** — unchanged reasoning from the original Sports pipeline: a neutral "vs." title is self-explanatory with two named option buttons (`OptionsTicket` renders each option's label as its own row) but carries no meaning at all next to a bare Yes/No pair, since `MarketExplainer`'s `yes_no` branch renders nothing by design (the title *is* the explanation). Publish is idempotent via the pick row's own `status` column, not a markets-table dedupe check the way the old pipeline used title/`closes_at` matching — a rerun sees `status = 'published'` already and skips. A bye week (no candidates at all) is stamped `status = 'skipped'` rather than erroring. A single market always resolves to the named `market_opened` push via `_notify_system_markets_created`'s own n=1 branch, called the same way every other pipeline create path does.
- **`sports-resolve-markets`** (kept, trimmed) polls The Odds API's `/scores` for each league independently against its own group (`NFL`/`CFB`, not one shared "Sports" group looping three leagues) and resolves the matching market by reconstructing the exact title `sports-weekly-publish` used — an exact lookup, not a parse, same as before. Since it's `multiple_choice`, resolving means finding the winning team's own `market_options` row and passing `p_option_id`, falling back to `p_outcome = 'void'` on a tie. Still every 6 hours (`20260830100000_reduce_sports_resolve_frequency.sql`'s schedule, untouched) — cheap regardless now, with only ever two live markets platform-wide instead of a whole league's slate.
- **`_prune_resolved_system_markets()` keeps NFL/CFB's history 20 deep, not 10** (`20260906103000`) — at one market a week, 10 was barely 10 weeks; 20 is roughly a full NFL season's worth of past picks still browsable.

**Both pipeline groups' hubs render `PipelineGroupFeed` (`components/groups/PipelineGroupFeed.tsx`) instead of the ordinary `GroupMarketSections` tabs every other group uses.** One "featured" slot always shows the group's most recent system market, whatever its actual status — open (still betting), closed (kickoff passed, no result yet), or already resolved/voided — with everything older listed as plain settled rows below it. The featured slot itself never falls back to an empty "come back later" placeholder: the group hub page (`app/(app)/groups/[groupId]/page.tsx`) picks `buckets.open[0] ?? buckets.awaiting_resolution[0]` first, and only falls back to the newest settled market (`settledPage.markets[0]`) when neither exists — so the moment a market resolves, the same slot just keeps showing it with its real outcome until the next one replaces it, rather than blanking out until the next scheduled run. `featured === null` only for a genuinely brand-new pipeline group that has never created a market at all (a plain "no game yet" `EmptyState`, not a specific "come back Tuesday" promise — an earlier version of this page tried a `publicGroupWaitingCopy()` helper with that exact copy, removed the same day live use showed a market simply going quiet-then-resolved read better as "still showing you what actually happened" than as an empty placeholder).

**A check-then-insert existence guard isn't safe against two concurrent invocations — found for real 2026-09-08** when two identical NFL "Seahawks vs. Patriots" markets got created within moments of each other. `sports-weekly-publish` reads `game_of_week_picks.status` and only writes `'published'` back after creating the market, so two concurrent runs can both see `awaiting_pick`/`picked` and both publish — most likely a network-level retry reaching the Edge Function as two separate, fully-independent HTTP requests, since `cron.job_run_details` confirms pg_cron itself only ever dispatched once. `markets_system_market_active_group_closes_at_key` (`20260910120000`) closes the actual gap with a database-level backstop instead of another application-level check: a partial unique index on `(group_id, closes_at)` for `is_system_market` rows still `open`/`closed`, so a second concurrent insert now fails with a real `23505` (`unique_violation`) instead of quietly succeeding. `sports-weekly-publish` catches that specific code and treats it as "a concurrent request already won this" (`continue`s past the league), not a real failure, leaving the winning request's own update/notify as the only one that ran. Scoped to `open`/`closed` rather than every status so it never has to reconcile against old resolved/voided history.

**NFL/CFB mods (and the owner) can hand-create a market too, additive to whatever the pipeline auto-creates.** `create_market()` briefly had an unconditional check (`20260828130000`) rejecting the group by name before its usual public-group mod-or-owner check even ran; `20260830180000` removed that carve-out, so these groups use the exact same mod-or-owner gate as any other public group, and `BottomNav`'s "+" button / `/markets/new` dropped their matching `system_pipeline` blocked-reason branch back to the ordinary `not_moderator` one. Safe with respect to `_prune_resolved_system_markets()`'s and `expire_stale()`'s pipeline-only sweeps because both key off `is_system_market = true`, and `create_market()` never sets that column — a hand-created market can never be counted against or pruned alongside the pipeline's own.

**Notification volume on these groups needed four separate fixes, all found from watching the pipeline run against real data:**

- **Nobody gets an odds or resolution push for a market they never bet on, in any public group.** `get_event_recipients()`'s catch-all branch (`market_closed`, `resolution_proposed`, `resolution_challenged`, `market_resolved`, `market_voided`, `criteria_updated`) additionally requires the recipient to have a bet on that specific market whenever the market's group `is_public` — a directory-joined public group can run into the hundreds of members, and most of them never touch any one specific game. `market_opened` is deliberately exempt: it's the invitation to bet in the first place, sent before anyone could possibly have a bet on it yet.
- **A single pipeline run that creates several markets at once collapses into one push, not one per market.** The old continuously-polling Sports pipeline used to create several markets in one run; NFL/CFB's weekly publish only ever creates one, but the mechanism stays in place for whenever more than one clears at once. `_create_system_market()` doesn't emit `market_opened` itself — each Edge Function collects the ids of whatever it actually created during its own run and calls `_notify_system_markets_created(p_group_id, p_market_ids)` once at the end: exactly one market emits the normal named `market_opened` push, two or more emit a single `system_markets_opened` event instead. `system_markets_opened` is its own `notification_event_type` value with no `market_id` required.
- **No underdog-win push, and no premature "proposed" push, on any pipeline group.** `impressive_bet` reads as a real, rare milestone in a small group of friends — in a directory-joined group, it's routine noise, so `_finalize_market_core()` (`20260830260000`) skips emitting it whenever the market's group `is_public`. Separately, a public group's `propose_resolution()` already calls `finalize_market()` in the same transaction as the proposal — `propose_resolution()` (`20260830270000`) skips `resolution_proposed` entirely for a public group's market instead of emitting a push whose "you have N hours to challenge it" claim is simply false there.
- **Neither inactivity nudge fires on a public group either** (`20260910100000`). `send_nudges()`'s two loops (`weekend_nudge`'s "anyone up to something worth betting on?" and `market_closing_soon`'s "it's been a minute since your last one") both gained `and not g.is_public`: a directory-joined pipeline group going quiet between scheduled runs isn't a friend group losing momentum, it's just the pipeline's own cadence, and members were seeing this as "it's been a while" noise on NFL/CFB specifically. Same reasoning as the bullet above, just found later.

Every pipeline function shares `_create_system_market()` / `_resolve_system_market()` and `pipeline_settings` (the admin kill switch, one row for `'sports'` covering NFL/CFB's weekly-prepare/weekly-publish/resolve) — both described in "Postgres functions" above — and `_record_sweep_failure()` for per-row error isolation, keyed by a deterministic hash of whatever external id the failure is about (a league/week or an event) so retries upsert onto the same `sweep_failures` row instead of each looking like a brand-new failure. Cron (`20260906104000` retires the old `barbets-sports-create-markets` entry and adds the two weekly ones; `20260912091138` unschedules two more cron entries left over from a second pipeline that used to run alongside this one, now retired): `barbets-sports-weekly-prepare` Monday, `barbets-sports-weekly-publish` Tuesday, `barbets-sports-resolve-markets` every 6 hours — all disabled on staging along with every other scheduled job, same guarded `cron.schedule()`/`cron.unschedule()` + `exception when others` pattern as `send-push`'s own scheduling migration. **Sports resolving every 6 hours rather than 30 minutes dates to `20260830100000_reduce_sports_resolve_frequency.sql`**: every `sports-resolve-markets` run calls The Odds API's `/scores` once per league, which spends usage credits (`/events` does not) — every-30-minutes across what was then 3 leagues was 144 requests/day, enough to exhaust a typical free-tier ~500/month budget in about a day (`OUT_OF_USAGE_CREDITS`, 2026-08-26). With only 2 leagues and one market a week each now, the credit pressure that forced this cadence in the first place is far lower, but the interval was never revisited since it costs nothing to leave alone. A completed game more than `DAYS_FROM` (2 days) in the past by the time credits/cadence allow a successful poll falls out of the `/scores` lookback window and never auto-resolves — that market needs a moderator's ordinary hand-resolve through the UI. Every market any pipeline function creates is stamped `markets.is_system_market = true` (`_create_system_market()`), which is also what stops `expire_stale()`'s zero-bet auto-void from swallowing a pipeline market nobody happened to bet on before its resolve Edge Function ever ran. Each function also calls `_record_pipeline_run()` once per invocation, feeding `list_pipeline_health()`'s admin-panel healthy/stale/failing badge.

**`20260830300000`'s multiple_choice widening briefly broke the `is_system_market = true` stamp entirely**, worth remembering as a general lesson even though the specific pipeline it hit (the old Sports) no longer exists in this shape: its rewrite of `_create_system_market()`'s two `insert` statements dropped `is_system_market` from both column lists, so every market either branch created after it deployed silently took the column's own default (`false`) instead — a plain body edit with no signature change, so nothing about the function-change rule caught it. Found 2026-08-31 via a full morning's batch getting auto-voided within a second of each market's own `closes_at`, zero bets, before the resolve function ever got a chance to look. Fixed same-day by `20260831100000_fix_create_system_market_missing_flag.sql`. **A widening migration that touches a function's `insert` column list is exactly as risky as one that touches its signature, just invisible to the `DROP FUNCTION` rule that catches the signature case** — the checklist for "did I actually keep every column the old body set" has no automated backstop here. A second, related regression on the same function (`20260903150000`) is worth knowing about too: `20260830300000` was itself redeclared from a base that predated `20260828130000`'s removal of `_create_system_market()`'s own direct `market_opened` emit, silently reinstating a double-push (once from the function itself, once from `_notify_system_markets_created()`) that persisted across two more migrations before anyone noticed — the same "redeclared from a stale base" failure mode this doc already tracks for `create_market()` and `update_group_settings()`.

**A market stuck past the resolve lookback window doesn't just sit there quietly — it silently kills the whole pipeline**, and this lesson is exactly why the new weekly design keeps a safety net even though the specific counting mechanism that surfaced it (an `OPEN_MARKET_CAP` the old continuous Sports pipeline checked before creating anything new) no longer applies to NFL/CFB. Found 2026-09-01: a transient Odds API 502 at resolve time meant a batch of games weren't picked up before they aged past `DAYS_FROM` (2 days), permanently stranding them in `closed` with no automatic path to resolution and silently zeroing out the old pipeline's create output for two straight days — "0 created, 0 failed," indistinguishable from a quiet day with no games. `20260901100000_void_permanently_stuck_system_markets.sql` adds a standing `expire_stale()` sweep (`system_market_stuck_closed`) that force-voids any `is_system_market` row still `closed` 3 days past `closed_at`, via the same `_void_market()` escape hatch `wind_down_void` uses elsewhere in that function — comfortably past every pipeline's normal resolve cadence, bounding how long one stuck market can block anything downstream without trying to resolve it correctly (that still needs a human).

`.edu`-style campus membership gating remains scoped but not built. Campus groups ship open (no verification) until real usage shows that's a problem worth a new email-sending pipeline for.

## PWA & push

- `public/manifest.json`: `start_url`/`scope` are both `"/"` (not a page that requires auth — Android's install criteria need a reachable start URL), standard + maskable icons.
- Maskable icons must be a full-bleed square with a *solid* background (no transparency, no pre-baked rounded corners) — Android applies its own mask shape at display time, and content needs to sit within the inner ~80% "safe zone." A rounded, padded source image shows up with blank corners once Android's own mask is applied over it.
- The Android push **badge** (small status-bar icon) is a *separate*, dedicated asset (`badge-mono.png`) — Android renders it as a solid-tint silhouette using only the alpha channel, ignoring color entirely. The regular full-color app icon is used for the larger in-notification `icon` field, which platforms that support it still show in color.
- `public/sw.js`: network-first for everything (this app is almost entirely dynamic — a stale cached market page could show wrong odds), with a resilient `Promise.allSettled` app-shell precache so one failed asset can't block install. Bump `CACHE_NAME` whenever `SHELL_URLS` changes.
- **Offline (web/PWA)**: a failed navigation (`event.request.mode === 'navigate'`) falls back to the precached `/offline` page (`app/offline/page.tsx`) instead of a stale snapshot of `/` — that used to be the fallback, and for a logged-in visitor a cached copy of the logged-out marketing page read as a broken app, not "you're offline." `/offline` has to render correctly from the exact HTML precached at install time, so it does no server data fetching, no auth check, and no `next/image` (its optimization endpoint is a network request the service worker hasn't cached, so it'd render as a broken image offline) — a plain `<img>` against an already-precached asset instead, same reasoning `RevealTicket.tsx` uses. It also shows a read-only "last known balances" list, sourced from `lib/offlineSnapshot.ts`'s localStorage snapshot (one `{groupId, groupName, balance, savedAt}` row per group, written by `SaveBalanceSnapshot` whenever the group hub page loads) — the one piece of app data cached client-side, deliberately never odds or bet state, and always labeled "may be out of date." This only ever helps once the service worker has actually installed from a prior online visit; there's no fallback for a cold load that's offline from the very first visit. `OfflineHeadline` splits the copy on `navigator.onLine`: "You're offline" when the device genuinely has no connection (the common case this page was built for), but "We're having trouble, this is on us" when the device has a live connection and the request still failed — the original wording read as wrong during the 2026-08-29 Supabase degradation below, where every visitor had a working connection and the app just wasn't answering. `public/offline.html` (the native fallback, next bullet) mirrors the same split inline since it can't import the React component.
- **`/offline`'s own precache is not guaranteed, and the old fallback chain had no answer for that.** The service worker's `Promise.allSettled` install (a few bullets up) means one failed asset — including `/offline` itself — can't block install, but that also means `/offline` can be the one that's missing, and a service worker that first installs *during* an outage is exactly when that's likeliest. Before `fallbackOfflineResponse()` existed, a failed navigation with no cached `/offline` fell through to `Response.error()`, an opaque network-error response with no page behind it — the browser logs "resulted in a network error response" and the tab just dead-ends, invisible to `#errors` the same way `ChunkErrorRecovery`'s bug was (nothing threw; the request just never resolved into anything worth reporting). `public/sw.js` now carries a second, fully inline copy of the offline page (can't depend on the cache having anything, by definition) as the true last resort, so a failed navigation always resolves to a real page. Learned this the hard way on 2026-08-29, when Supabase's API gateway degraded for several hours: every request through `proxy.ts` touches Supabase to refresh the session, so the slowdown reached every page load, not just data-heavy ones, and browser tabs that tried to load `/login` during the worst of it hit exactly this dead end.
- **Offline (native/Capacitor)**: the web fallback above doesn't reach the native shell — Android's WebView doesn't support service-worker interception of the top-level navigation request at all (only sub-resource requests), so a cold app-open with no connection never reaches the service worker and instead shows the WebView's own generic connection-error screen. `capacitor.config.ts`'s `server.errorPath: 'offline.html'` is Capacitor's own native hook for this (`BridgeWebViewClient.onReceivedError`/`onReceivedHttpError` on Android, `didFailProvisionalNavigation` on iOS): it loads a page bundled locally from `webDir` with zero network. `public/offline.html` is that page — fully self-contained (inline CSS, no React/Next.js/next/font, since none of that build output is available to a natively-bundled static file), synced into both native projects via `npx cap sync`. This needs a real native build/store release to reach users, unlike everything else in this section, which a web deploy alone covers.
- **`errorPath` only fires on an explicit WebView load failure, not a load that just hangs — and a hang is indistinguishable from the boot splash never coming down.** `MainActivity.java`'s `WatchdogWebViewClient` (Android only, replacing the bridge's default `BridgeWebViewClient` via `bridge.setWebViewClient(...)` right after `super.onCreate()`) starts a 10-second timer on every main-frame `onPageStarted` and forces the same `offline.html` fallback (`view.loadUrl(bridge.getErrorUrl())`) if the page hasn't finished, errored, or HTTP-errored by then; the timer is cancelled on any of those three outcomes, and never scheduled for a load of `offline.html` itself. Without this, a stalled request — no error, no completion, which is what a filtering or badly degraded cellular connection looks like — leaves `BootSplash.tsx`'s native splash (`launchAutoHide: false`) up forever, because nothing releases it until the page's own JS runs, and that JS never arrives. Reported 2026-09-10: UK Android users stuck on the boot splash on cellular only, wifi unaffected, with the same partial failure (missing image, failed login) reproducible in a plain Chrome tab on the same connection — pointing at carrier-side content filtering of the domain rather than a server or code issue, which this watchdog can't fix but at least fails open into a retryable screen instead of an indefinite splash. iOS has the same theoretical gap (`didFailProvisionalNavigation` has the same "only fires on an explicit error" limitation) but no watchdog yet, since it hasn't been reported there.
- **The native shells run current JS against a plugin layer that can be months old, and that asymmetry breaks things in one specific way.** `capacitor.config.ts` sets `server.url` to the deployed app, so a Vercel deploy reaches every install immediately — the web layer is never stale. The *plugin* layer only moves when someone ships a store build. So **any web release that reaches for a newly-added Capacitor plugin runs against installs that don't have it**, and the bridge answers with an `UNIMPLEMENTED` rejection rather than the plugin's real result. This has already happened once: the day `@capacitor/share`/`@capacitor/filesystem` were added for image sharing, every existing Android install started running the new share code against a shell with neither plugin, and the button just read "Try again". `lib/shareImage.ts`'s `isMissingPlugin()` translates that class of rejection into "Update the app from the store" instead of surfacing `"Share" plugin is not implemented on android`. **Adding a plugin is a store release, not a deploy** — and until that release lands, the feature needs a graceful answer for the installs that don't have it yet.
- iOS never fires `beforeinstallprompt`. `usePushSubscription`'s `platform === 'ios-needs-install'` detects iOS + not-yet-installed, and the "All notifications" master row locks its switch and says to install to the home screen first rather than firing a permission prompt that can't work; this triggers automatically, nothing to configure.
- **Browser "add to home screen" install instructions are gone** (`InstallPrompt.tsx` on `/profile/account`, `InstallBanner.tsx` mounted app-wide — both deleted). The product direction moved toward the native app (and, eventually, deep-linking mobile browser traffic to it — see the note below) rather than a mobile-web PWA install as the goal, so promoting a browser home-screen install worked against that. `useInstallPrompt.ts` (the platform/`beforeinstallprompt` detection those two shared) stays, because `PushReminderModal` still needs its `platform === 'installed'` signal — see below. The "All notifications" master row at the top of `NotificationPreferences` (on `/profile/notifications`) remains the durable place to opt in/out of push.
- `PushReminderModal` (mounted app-wide in `app/(app)/layout.tsx`, not on any one page, so it's visible regardless of which page a member happens to land on first) used to exist, get removed for being an app-wide interrupt, then get reinstated — the actual complaint wasn't "it's a modal," it's that it opened and then immediately closed itself for anyone already subscribed: `platform` flips to `'ready'` synchronously on mount while the real subscribed/not-subscribed answer is still an in-flight promise, so any consumer gating on `platform` alone reads the still-default `subscribed === false` for one tick. `usePushSubscription`'s `subscribedKnown` flag exists solely so a consumer can wait for that promise instead of the synchronous placeholder. It's also gated on `useInstallPrompt`'s `platform === 'installed'`, not just push-API support — Chrome (desktop or Android) will grant Web Push permission from an ordinary browser tab with nothing installed, but a subscription made there has none of the reliability of one made from an actual home-screen install, so asking is deferred until the user has committed to installing. The modal opens right after joining/creating a group (`JUST_JOINED_GROUP_KEY` in `localStorage`, set by `JoinFlow`/`CreateGroupForm` immediately before their redirect so it survives the navigation — relevant once already installed, e.g. joining a second group from the installed app) and otherwise at most once every `REPROMPT_DAYS` (7) on an ordinary app open — never for a `permission === 'denied'` device, since `requestPermission()` there just instantly re-returns `'denied'` with nothing the user can do about it from inside the app.
- VAPID private key lives only as a Supabase Edge Function secret, never in Next.js env or the client bundle. Same posture for the FCM service account key (`FCM_SERVICE_ACCOUNT_JSON_B64`, Capacitor/native push) — a Supabase secret only, never shipped client-side.
- **`ChunkErrorRecovery.tsx`, mounted first in `app/layout.tsx`, force-reloads the tab once on a stale JS chunk load failure.** Every Vercel deploy replaces the previous build's chunk manifest outright, so a tab that was already open across a deploy (a backgrounded PWA, or a browser tab left sitting on an old page) can ask for a chunk that no longer exists the moment it makes a client-side navigation, throwing `ChunkLoadError`. That throw happens inside webpack's own dynamic-import machinery, not inside a React render, so it reaches neither `global-error.tsx` (a render-time error boundary) nor `instrumentation.ts`'s `onRequestError` (a server-side hook) — it was, until this component existed, both invisible to this app's error reporting and unrecoverable for the user, who was simply stuck wherever they were (often the boot splash, since BootSplash's own hide timers still fire but nothing behind them ever painted). Learned this the hard way on 2026-08-28: three merges to `main` landed within about two hours, and the resulting run of back-to-back deploys hit enough already-open sessions at once that it read as "the app is down for everyone," with zero trace in `#errors` because the failure mode structurally can't reach it. The fix listens for `error`/`unhandledrejection` globally and reloads on a `ChunkLoadError`-shaped one, which fetches the current HTML and its current manifest and self-heals. A `sessionStorage` cooldown stops a genuinely broken deployment from reload-looping the tab forever.
- **A service worker registration outlives the site that created it, and a 404 does not clean it up.** This matters because `mybarbets.com` served this app (and therefore registered this `sw.js`, cache `barbets-shell-v7`) before becoming the marketing site. Per the spec's Update algorithm a non-ok script response rejects the update job and only removes the registration when nothing is installed — the proposal to make 404/410 unregister was closed wontfix (w3c/ServiceWorker#204). Serving nothing at `/sw.js` would therefore leave the old worker controlling that origin indefinitely, still firing app push notifications whose relative `data.url` values 404 there. So `barbets-www` serves a deliberate *farewell* worker at that path instead: it wipes all caches, has **no `fetch` handler at all** (a registration without one cannot intercept navigations, so it provably cannot shadow the marketing pages), discards any push payload and shows one `tag`-collapsed "Barbets has moved" notification, and unregisters itself plus its push subscription on a date baked into the file. That last part also cleans up the stale rows for free: the dead endpoint starts returning 404/410, and `send-push` already deletes on those. Nothing on the marketing site ever calls `navigator.serviceWorker.register()`, so no new visitor gets a worker.
- Inside the Capacitor shell, `usePushSubscription.ts` branches on `Capacitor.isNativePlatform()` to a completely different registration path (`@capacitor-firebase/messaging`'s `requestPermissions()`/`getToken()` instead of the Web Push API) rather than trying to make one code path cover both — the two have almost nothing in common (permission model, subscription object shape, how you detect an existing subscription on mount). Both still funnel into the same `push_subscriptions` table and the same `setNotificationsEnabled()` toggle, so the "All notifications" switch doesn't need to know which path is live.
- **Saving a subscription goes through `save_push_subscription()`/`save_native_push_subscription()` (`20260831110000_claim_push_subscription_on_save.sql`), not a plain client upsert, because an endpoint or FCM token identifies a device, not a Barbets account.** `push_subscriptions` is unique per `(user_id, endpoint)`/`(user_id, fcm_token)` only, so if a second account ever subscribes from the same physical device/app-install an earlier account subscribed from and neither ever unsubscribes, both rows persist indefinitely — and any push correctly addressed to *either* account's `user_id` lands on that one shared device regardless of which account it was actually meant for. Found in production 2026-08-31: a group's own member correctly received a push, and it also arrived on a second, unrelated account that had registered the same FCM token from the same phone over a month earlier and never cleared it — not a `get_event_recipients` bug, the recipient list was correct both times, the device itself just answered to two accounts. `lib/actions/push.ts`'s `savePushSubscription`/`saveNativePushSubscription` now call these SECURITY DEFINER functions instead of upserting the table directly (the table's own `push_subscriptions_all_own` policy still allows a plain client upsert/delete of a user's *own* rows, which is why `removePushSubscription`/`removeNativePushSubscription` didn't need to change) — each function deletes any *other* user's row sharing the exact endpoint/token being presented before writing the caller's own, so whoever most recently proves they hold that credential (only the device itself can produce it) claims it. Four pre-existing stale rows from before this fix were deleted by hand in production the same day.
- **Zoom is disabled at the viewport, not by auditing every input's font size.** `app/layout.tsx`'s `viewport` export sets `maximumScale: 1, userScalable: false` (and `public/offline.html`'s own `<meta viewport>` matches it). The native iOS shell is a WKWebView, which zooms the page the same way Mobile Safari does the moment focus lands on a text input styled under 16px — and this app has plenty of those (compact settings fields, inline nickname/season-name editors), by deliberate design choice for those surfaces. Rather than bumping every one of those inputs to 16px+ (which would fight the intended visual sizing across a lot of components), the viewport itself refuses to scale at all, which is also just correct for something that's meant to feel like a native app screen rather than a document you'd pinch-zoom to read. This reaches existing installs on the next load since the native shell loads the real deployed origin (`capacitor.config.ts`'s `server.url`), not a bundled copy — a web deploy is enough, no store release needed.

## Testing

`npm test` runs Vitest integration tests directly against a real hosted Supabase project — no local Docker/mocks. Which project depends on credential precedence, resolved in `loadTestEnv()`: `.env.local` < `.env.test.local` < real environment variables, for `NEXT_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_URL` / `NEXT_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_ANON_KEY` / `SUPABASE_SERVICE_ROLE_KEY`.

The middle layer is the one that matters day to day. `.env.local` must keep pointing at production so `npm run dev` shows real data, but the suite creates and deletes ~110 users per run and should not be doing that in the live project. Putting the staging keys in `.env.test.local` (gitignored by the existing `.env*` rule) sends every `npm test` to staging with no flags and no extra command, and leaves the dev server alone; delete the file and tests go back to production. CI has neither file and injects real environment variables, which is why those win outright. `.bulk_cleanup.mjs` deliberately resolves credentials the same way, since a sweep script that follows a different project to the tests is worse than none.

`tests/integration/helpers/testUsers.ts` creates real users via the admin API, signs each in for a real access token, and every test cleans up everything it created (including any group it made a user own — group ownership has no cascading delete, so cleanup order matters) so nothing leaks into the live project. `setupGroup()` (in `tests/integration/helpers/scenarios.ts`) derives each test user's nickname from their `TestUser.tag`, since every test's `create_group`/`join_group` calls route through it — that's the one place a suite-wide nickname requirement gets satisfied. Covers the full privacy/money acceptance checklist: subject invisibility at every market status, parimutuel conservation under randomized/adversarial bet distributions, notification recipient exclusion, balance-capped bet sizing, season rollover. `tests/integration/markets.multiple_choice.test.ts` covers the same checklist for `multiple_choice`: N-option parimutuel conservation, per-option subject invisibility (and visibility at reveal), the union subject cap, hedged bets across options, and the `bets_side_xor_option` constraint rejecting a bet with both/neither of `side`/`option_id` set. `tests/integration/nicknames.test.ts` covers the nickname model itself: join-time collisions, rejoin preserving the existing nickname, and `update_nickname`'s collision/format/non-member checks. `tests/integration/money.payout_distribution.test.ts` covers the `distribute_payout` opt-in: the creator cut (there is no endorser cut, see the money rules above), `bonus_pool` topping up another open market and being absorbed on that market's own resolution, the no-other-open-market fallback holding in `groups.pending_bonus_pool` rather than refunding this market's own bettors, a `bonus_pool`-carrying market voiding with nowhere else to send it (also holds in `pending_bonus_pool`, owner balance untouched), and that pool seeding the next market created (`bonus_pool`/`carried_bonus_pool` both set, pool zeroed). `tests/integration/money.void_by_owner.test.ts` covers the owner kill switch: the non-owner rejection, exact refunds, `market_voided` (never `market_resolved`) and its recipient set, the already-settled rejection, the 404-not-403 for an owner who is the market's subject, and the bonus-pool conservation regression described in the design-decisions note about `refund_all_bets()`. `tests/integration/invite_code_rate_limit.test.ts` covers the invite-code guess limit: ten misses spend the budget, the eleventh is refused (and isn't itself counted, since it never reaches the lookup), the budget is shared with `join_group` so a preview spree closes off joining with a real code, a lapsed window restores normal service, `join_group` returns zero rows rather than raising for a code matching nothing, a code that really exists never counts however often it's used, and the counter table is unreadable by the client it counts.
