  - **Editing is its own route** (`/groups/[groupId]/settings/edit`), not an expansion inside the list, and there is deliberately no autosave: every change is a draft until Save, which is the only thing that makes Cancel mean anything. The controls are grouped into Money / Starting a market / Settling it / Seasons & time, and the save bar is `fixed` above `--bottomnav-height` the same way `BetslipBar` stacks itself. A non-owner reaching the route gets `notFound()`, not a redirect.
- **Undefined Tailwind color steps fail silently, which is why `GroupDeletionBanner` looked like a caption for months.** The banner and the transfer-ownership confirm both reached for `bg-danger-50`, `border-danger-200` and `text-danger-600`. `app/globals.css`'s `@theme` block only defines `danger-100`, `danger-500` and `danger-700`, so Tailwind emitted nothing at all for those three classes and the most urgent message in the app rendered with no fill, no border and default body text. Nothing errors, nothing warns, and it reads as a deliberate understatement rather than a bug. The fix was to use the steps that exist rather than to invent three more; **before using a `color-NNN` step anywhere, check it against the `@theme` block**, since the palette is deliberately sparse (three danger steps, not ten) and a plausible-looking step number is not evidence it exists.
- **How it works is three tabs driven by a query param, not client state, and its "Your group" tab reads live settings.** `?tab=basics|your-group|edge-cases` on `/how-it-works`, so a deep link from a setting row (`?group=<id>&tab=your-group`) lands on the right tab and the back button behaves — the page stays a pure server component with no client JS for the tabbing at all. The "Your group" tab only exists when `?group=` resolved to a group the reader can actually see; without one there are two tabs, not a third full of hardcoded defaults pretending to be the group's real configuration. The old five-slide auto-advancing lifecycle carousel (`LifecycleSlideshow`, now deleted) is a static vertical timeline: five stages that only make sense in order shouldn't need four taps to be read as an order.
- **Storage is the one place access control isn't a Postgres RLS policy.** The `resolution-proofs` bucket (resolution proposal proof photos) is the app's only use of Supabase Storage, and deliberately carries no `storage.objects` policies at all — `storage.objects` isn't gated the same way a table row is, so a photo's bytes don't automatically inherit `resolution_proposals`' RLS the way a new column on that table would. Rather than write a second, parallel access-control system in storage policies, access is mediated entirely by Server Actions in `lib/actions/resolution.ts` that query `resolution_proposals`/`visible_markets` as the requesting user (real RLS, real gate) before ever using the service-role client to upload or mint a signed URL. If a second Storage bucket is ever added, default to this pattern over writing bucket-level RLS.
- **Adding a new trailing default parameter to a Postgres function does not replace an existing overload — and this has bitten this project six separate times.** Five were caught the hard way (most visibly `update_group_settings` picking up a fourth new parameter across three different features, then a fifth time when `20260903100000_backfill_season_id_on_seasons_enable.sql` redeclared it from a base that predated two later signature changes — `20260830240000`'s `p_prize_text`/`p_punishment_text` and `20260901180000`'s settings-audit body — creating a second overload instead of replacing it, and breaking every caller across the whole test suite until `20260903140000_fix_update_group_settings_overload_regression.sql` dropped the stale 15-arg version and merged the missing pieces back in); the sixth, `_cleanup_departing_member`, was found only by running the catalog query below and had been sitting in the database undetected for a month (`20260810120000_drop_stale_cleanup_departing_member_overload.sql` removes it). That one is the instructive case, because every symptom that normally exposes this was absent: all three callers passed the new parameter, so nothing errored, and the helper is internal and revoked from `authenticated`, so PostgREST never touched it. What made it worth finding anyway is that the orphaned body was a *stale money rule* — it unconditionally refunded a departing member's open bets, the exact behavior deliberately removed to close the peek-then-leave exploit. **The lesson: "nothing is broken" is not evidence there's no leftover overload. Only the catalog query is.** A function's identity in Postgres is its argument *type* signature, not its defaults — `CREATE OR REPLACE` with a longer signature creates a second overload alongside the old one, and PostgREST then can't pick a candidate for a call that omits the new parameter (and worse, the error only surfaces the next time someone calls it *without* the new param — a function only ever touched with the new signature looks completely fine until then). Every signature change needs an explicit `DROP FUNCTION <old signature>` for the superseded version in the *same migration* that adds the parameter, not a "add first, drop unlisted the moment it breaks" approach. After pushing, verify with a throwaway call passing every parameter by name (`supabase.rpc(name, {...all params...})`) — if PostgREST error-message-dumps two candidate signatures, an overload was left behind. The direct check, which doesn't depend on guessing which call site breaks, is to ask the catalog whether any function name has more than one signature:

```sql
-- Extension-owned functions (citext, pg_net, ...) legitimately overload and would
-- otherwise drown the result, so exclude anything that's an extension member.
select p.proname, count(*), array_agg(pg_get_function_identity_arguments(p.oid))
from pg_proc p
join pg_namespace n on n.oid = p.pronamespace
where n.nspname = 'public'
  and not exists (
    select 1 from pg_depend d
    where d.objid = p.oid and d.classid = 'pg_proc'::regclass and d.deptype = 'e'
  )
group by p.proname having count(*) > 1;
```

Anything this returns is either a deliberate overload (there are none in this project today) or a leftover that will fail the next time someone calls it the old way. As of `20260810162000` this returns zero rows across all 73 app-owned functions, verified after `update_group_settings` **lost** a parameter (`p_endorser_payout_pct`) rather than gaining one — the rule is identical in both directions, since a function's identity is its argument type list either way. Run it after any migration that changes a signature.
- **Redeclaring a large function from a stale base silently reverts whatever landed on top of it, and this has now bitten `create_market()` three separate times, plus a fourth on `_create_system_market()`.** Unlike the overload trap above (wrong *signature*), this one is wrong *body*: `CREATE OR REPLACE` with an unchanged signature is a true replace, so if the migration copying the body forward starts from anything other than the function's actual current definition, every change made between that stale base and now is silently discarded, with no error anywhere — the function still works, it just works like an older version of itself. `20260826100000_fix_create_market_regression.sql` was the first instance (a migration built on a base that predated two option/unit length-cap fixes, caught by CI). `20260828230000_reassert_create_market_pipeline_gate.sql` was the second, a staging-only variant of the same root cause (a shared staging database applying multiple PRs' migrations out of timestamp order via `--include-all`, so a branch cut before the Sports pipeline gate existed could clobber it on staging regardless of what merged to main). The third was this doc's own author: `20260829130000_lifecycle_event_instrumentation.sql` redeclared `create_market()` from `20260826100000` — genuinely the latest version at a glance, since it was the most recent file whose *name* mentioned `create_market` — without checking whether any *later* migration had also touched the function body under a different name. `20260828130000_public_group_pipeline_notifications.sql` had, adding the Sports hand-create gate two days after the file used as the base, and pushing the stale redeclare removed that gate from production outright until `20260829170000_fix_create_market_missing_pipeline_gate.sql` restored it (caught by the integration suite, not by review). The fourth hit `_create_system_market()` specifically: that same `20260828130000` migration also removed its direct `market_opened` emit, moving the decision to `_notify_system_markets_created()` (one named push for a single-market run, one consolidated `system_markets_opened` push for a multi-market run, so a multi-market pipeline run fires one push instead of several). `20260830300000_system_market_multiple_choice.sql` redeclared `_create_system_market()` from a base that predated that removal, reinstating the direct emit, and `20260831100000_fix_create_system_market_missing_flag.sql` copied that stale body forward again — so every Sports market from `20260830300000` onward double-pushed until `20260903150000_fix_create_system_market_stray_market_opened_emit.sql` deleted the line again, caught by the integration suite once staging's schema cache had been forced current, not by review. **The lesson: before redeclaring a function whose name suggests it changes rarely, grep for every migration that touches that function name specifically (not just ones with it in the filename) and use the chronologically latest one as the base — or better, pull the live definition directly** (`select pg_get_functiondef(oid) from pg_proc where proname = '<name>'`) **and diff your intended new version against that, rather than reconstructing it from migration-file archaeology.** A function that's been touched by several unrelated features (`create_market`, `update_group_settings`, `expire_stale`, `_create_system_market`) is exactly the shape most likely to have a change hiding in a migration whose title doesn't mention it.
- **`revoke ... from public` does not revoke from `anon`, and every function in this app had assumed it did.** Supabase's project bootstrap runs `alter default privileges ... grant execute on functions to anon, authenticated, service_role`, which grants `anon` its own direct, standing EXECUTE privilege on every new function the instant it's created — a separate grant from the `PUBLIC` pseudo-role, and untouched by revoking from `PUBLIC` afterward. This codebase's whole convention (every function ends with `revoke execute on function ... from public; grant execute on function ... to authenticated;`) was written on the assumption that the first line closes the door and the second opens exactly one. It closes the wrong door: `anon` walks straight through the standing bootstrap grant regardless of what `PUBLIC` has. Confirmed live rather than inferred: calling `get_group_by_invite_code` with nothing but the project's public anon key, no session, no signed-up account, executed successfully and returned the group's real name and avatar for any guessed code — and `_enforce_invite_code_rate_limit()` no-ops entirely when `auth.uid()` is null, so none of that guessing was even being counted. The account requirement the whole invite-code rate limit is built on was never actually enforced by Postgres for that function; `app/join/[code]/page.tsx`'s `if (!user) redirect('/login')` was the only thing standing in the way, and that's trivial to skip by calling PostgREST directly. Fixed by two migrations applied straight to production and only reconstructed into this repo afterward (`20260814110000_anon_invite_code_exists.sql`, `20260814120000_invite_functions_revoke_anon.sql` — see "Keeping this document current" below for why that gap existed and what it implies about staging). The fix is `revoke execute on function <name> from anon;` as an explicit third line, and `invite_code_exists()` — the one function that's actually supposed to answer something for a signed-out caller — is the one place that line is a `grant` instead. The audit query, same shape as the overload one above:

```sql
select p.proname, pg_get_function_identity_arguments(p.oid)
from pg_proc p
join pg_namespace n on n.oid = p.pronamespace
where n.nspname = 'public'
  and not exists (
    select 1 from pg_depend d
    where d.objid = p.oid and d.classid = 'pg_proc'::regclass and d.deptype = 'e'
  )
  and has_function_privilege('anon', p.oid, 'EXECUTE');
```

  This should return exactly two rows (`invite_code_exists`, `log_qr_scan`) — anything else is either a new, deliberate `anon` grant or the same mistake happening again. Worth running alongside the overload query after any migration that touches function grants.
- **The `anon`-grant fix above regressed silently for over two weeks, across nearly every function written since.** `20260814120000` fixed exactly the two functions it named and documented the general cause, but fixing named instances doesn't fix the underlying default — every function created afterward (the entire public-groups feature, awards, custom titles, seasons wind-down, both pipelines, roughly 90 functions across two weeks) got the same standing bootstrap grant to `anon` right back, because nobody was running the audit query the note above recommends. Caught by accident on `20260828`, adding `log_qr_scan()` and checking its own grant out of caution turned up 109 of ~117 app functions anon-executable on the live project. The practical exposure was narrower than the count suggests — most functions gate on `auth.uid()`, which is `NULL` for an anonymous caller, so the caller-identity check fails closed the same as a real logged-out stranger — but nothing had actually verified that for each of the 109 individually, and a function trusting a caller-supplied id instead of `auth.uid()` would have been a real, direct disclosure to anyone holding nothing but the public anon key. Fixed with one `do $$ ... $$` loop (`20260828170000`) revoking `anon` from every function the audit query flagged except the two meant to have it, rather than hand-auditing 109 signatures under time pressure. One function survived that loop anyway: `_compute_season_ends_at`, a pure `immutable` date-math helper that had *never* followed the revoke/grant convention at all (no table access, no `auth.uid()`, so nobody had thought it needed locking down) — it still carried Postgres's default create-time grant to `PUBLIC` itself, and `PUBLIC` privileges apply to every role including `anon`, so revoking from `anon` specifically cannot remove access `PUBLIC` still grants everyone. `20260828200000` closed that too, for consistency, even though the actual risk was negligible (pure function of its explicit arguments, no data, no side effects). **The lesson that matters going forward**: revoking from `PUBLIC` and revoking from `anon` are two different privileges that happen to produce the same practical effect for most functions (which have both revoked) but not all — a function missing either one is exploitable through whichever it's missing, and the only way to know is to actually run the audit query, not to assume the pattern held.
- **A migration applied straight to a production database and never committed is a gap the tooling won't let you ignore, which is the whole point of catching it this way.** The two migrations above existed only in `supabase_migrations.schema_migrations` on the live project — no matching file on any branch, ever. `npx supabase db push` refused to run anything at all until the gap was resolved (`LegacyDbPushMissingLocalError`), which is what surfaced this rather than it being noticed by inspection. The recorded `statements` column on that table held the exact SQL that had actually run, so the fix was to pull it verbatim and commit it as a real migration file rather than to reconstruct-and-hope or to `supabase migration repair --status reverted` the gap away — the latter only edits Supabase's own bookkeeping and would have made the history lie about what the database actually contains. The part worth remembering past this one incident: **CI only ever applies migrations that exist in this repo**, so a migration reaching production this way almost certainly never reached staging — the fix existed in production alone until the next PR's CI run backfilled it there too. Any time production and this repo's migration history disagree, assume staging is the one still exposed, not production.

To confirm an ambiguity without executing anything (these are money functions, so don't just call them and see): `explain select the_function(...)` **without** `analyze` plans the statement, which resolves the overload, but never runs the body. An ambiguous call fails at plan time with `42725 function ... is not unique`.
- **A foreign key to `users(id)` with no `on delete` clause silently blocks account deletion, and this was found across seven columns before the audit was actually complete.** `public.users.id` cascades from `auth.users` (`on delete cascade`), so `deleteAccount()`'s `admin.auth.admin.deleteUser()` call cascades into `public.users` — and Postgres's default `on delete` behavior for any *other* table's FK into `users(id)` is `no action`, which aborts that whole cascade the instant it reaches a table still holding a row. `notification_events.actor_id` was the first one found (via an unrelated CI flake investigation) and fixed to `on delete set null`; fixing it immediately exposed the second, `markets.creator_id`/`sponsor_id`, on the very next attempt to delete the same test user — which is what triggered a full audit rather than patching one column at a time as each new one surfaced. That audit found five more with the identical gap — `bets.user_id`, `votes.voter_id`, `challenges.challenger_id`, `resolution_proposals.proposer_id`, `resolution_clarifications.requester_id` — all now `on delete set null` too (`20260823110000_remaining_user_fk_set_null.sql`). `bets.user_id` is very likely the highest-impact fix in the whole set — it's set on every bet a user places, not a role most users only occupy occasionally (creating or sponsoring a market) — meaning a large share of real users who had ever placed a bet could not previously self-delete their account. Each column needed its own reasoning before being touched, not a blanket find-and-replace: `bets`/`votes`/`challenges`/`resolution_proposals`/`resolution_clarifications` rows are never deleted by `_cleanup_departing_member()` (it only refunds/voids, confirmed by reading its current body), so a settled, already-processed historical row going to `on delete set null` is safe by the same reasoning as `notification_events.actor_id` — and every one of those five columns was `not null`, so each needed `drop not null` before the FK could be recreated (a `not null` column can never receive the `null` an `on delete set null` action tries to write). The audit's sixth candidate, `market_subjects.user_id`, is part of a **composite primary key** (`market_id`, `user_id`) — a primary-key column can never be null, which would have ruled out `set null` outright — but it turned out to already be `on delete cascade` from its original migration, needing no fix; worth remembering as the shape a future genuinely-still-broken PK-member column would need (removing the row entirely, not nulling a piece of it). The general lesson, worth checking after any future table gains a `references users(id)` column: **decide the `on delete` behavior at the same time as the column, don't leave it to default** — `no action` is silently the worst option for a column that will eventually outlive the account it points to, and the failure mode (a generic "failed to delete, try again" error with no hint why) gives the person hitting it nothing to act on.
- **Hedging is opt-out per group, not a global rule.** `group_settings.allow_hedged_bets` defaults to on (today's parimutuel behavior, unchanged for every existing group) specifically to close a real exploit: betting big on a heavy favorite and a token amount on the underdog manufactures a favorable risk profile regardless of outcome. The enforcement lives in `place_bet` itself; `BetslipBar`'s client-side block (disabling the confirm button when a selection conflicts with an existing bet) is just a courtesy, same relationship every other business rule here has to its UI.
- **`BetslipBar` replaced the old inline `PlaceBetCard`** with a persistent bottom bar, styled like the reveal ticket, that slides up into a confirmation sheet instead of a form sitting mid-page. The point is a bettor always sees a clear stub (side, amount, balance after) before confirming, not a form that just resets silently on submit — a successful bet hands off to a full-screen success view (small checkmark animation, respects `prefers-reduced-motion`) before returning to the refreshed market page. Quick-amount chips are 1%/5%/10% of `group_settings.seed_amount` (the tokens a new member starts with), rounded to the nearest 5, not a percentage of current balance — balance swings with every bet, seed amount doesn't, so it's the stable reference point for "a small/medium/large bet in this group."
- **Tailwind v4's `space-y-*` puts margin on the bottom of every child except the literal last one, not margin-top on all-but-first like v3.** A `position: fixed` element still receives that margin even though it's out of normal flow, and for a `bottom-0` element an unwanted margin-bottom shows up as a real gap between it and the true screen edge. This bit `BetslipBar` for real: it renders a Fragment of four top-level elements (an in-flow spacer, the closed bar, a backdrop, and the sheet) into a `space-y-*` container, and the *sheet* ends up as the container's actual last child, not the bar — so only the bar got the stray margin, which is exactly why it floated while the sheet always sat flush. Confirmed by reproducing both states (24px gap without a fix, 0px with) before shipping the fix: every top-level element `BetslipBar` renders now carries an explicit `!m-0`, rather than relying on which one happens to land last in the DOM.
- **The market page's "Closes in" stat tile signals urgency with a breathing danger-colored border, not honey.** Honey is already this app's one accent color (the "Line" tile for over/under markets, every primary button), so reusing it to also mean "hurry up" would blur two different meanings into one color — `danger` (already used for form errors) reads as "pay attention" without colliding with it. `ClosesInStatTile.tsx` computes the under-1h threshold client-side on the same 30s tick `CountdownTimer` already uses, rather than a value frozen at page load, so it turns on live for anyone watching a market close in real time instead of requiring a refresh. The border (`.animate-urgent-breathe` in `globals.css`) fades to a static `danger-500` outline under `prefers-reduced-motion`, same posture as the bet-confirmed checkmark and `RevealTicket`'s capture guard.
- **A season ending doesn't mean every in-flight market gets discarded, but it also doesn't wait forever.** The old `end_season()` force-voided anything non-terminal the instant it ran, which meant a market someone had already started resolving (proposed, maybe already challenged) got thrown away along with everything else. `winding_down` splits the two cases: nothing-started-yet force-voids immediately (there's nothing to wait on), already-in-flight gets to finish through its normal timers, capped `resolution_window_hours` past season end so a stuck vote can't hold a season open indefinitely. The three call sites that can trigger the actual archive (`end_season`'s synchronous fast path, `finalize_market`'s tail hook, `expire_stale`'s hard-cap sweep) all funnel through one shared `_finalize_season()` helper, same "one place the logic lives" instinct as the multiple-choice payout unification above.
- **`/demo` is a sandbox, not a preview of real data, and is the default landing page for a brand-new account.** `signUp()`/`confirmSignup()` (`lib/actions/auth.ts`) send a freshly confirmed signup to `/demo` rather than `/groups` (an explicit `next`, e.g. an invite link's `/join/XXXX`, still overrides it) — a brand-new signed-up user with zero groups has nothing to do until someone joins them, so this is their one guaranteed first stop rather than an empty groups hub. `app/(app)/groups/page.tsx`'s own empty state links there too via a "Try a live demo" button, for anyone who lands on `/groups` some other way (signing in fresh with no memberships, say). Either way it's a fully client-side, no-Supabase-calls walkthrough (`components/demo/*`, `lib/demoScenario.ts`) of one fake yes/no market through six steps — open → closed odds → proposed → a contested vote → settled → where things live in the app (bottom nav tabs, @mention markets, seasons, public groups). It always resolves in the visitor's favor so the payout moment lands every time, but the payout itself runs through the real parimutuel formula (`floor(bet.amount * total_pool / winning_pool)`) against a seeded fake pool, so the number is genuinely computed, not scripted. Each step carries one coaching line under the market card, changing as the market moves, teaching *why* that step works the way it does rather than narrating what's on screen ahead of time. Deliberately doesn't touch `is_market_visible()`, `place_bet`, or any other real function — the contested-vote step and the proof chip are local stand-ins for `MarketActions`/`ResolutionProofButton`, which call server actions this fake market has no backing rows for. This used to be two separate things — a six-slide `OnboardingCarousel` shown once before ever reaching `/demo`, then a four-step walkthrough that skipped both a contested resolution and the vote — merged into one flow so nothing is explained before it has actually happened on screen; `OnboardingCarousel` no longer exists.
- **Navigation moved from a top `AppHeader` to a fixed bottom bar (`components/layout/BottomNav.tsx`), and Inbox was deleted outright rather than re-homed.** Four icon tabs (Home, Markets, Board, You) plus a contextual `+`; Home doesn't navigate, it opens a floating group switcher, and the `+` opens a "New market" type picker in-group or a "New group" teaser outside one. `BottomNav` is deliberately route-derived, not prop-driven: `lib/navRoute.ts`'s pure `getActiveNavTab`/`getRouteGroupId`/`shouldHideBottomNav` read the pathname to figure out the active tab, the current group, and whether to render at all, so none of the ~15 pages under `(app)/` need to thread nav state down manually. It's mounted as a sibling of `PullToRefresh`/`PageTransition` in `app/(app)/layout.tsx`, not nested inside either — both wrap `{children}` in a div that sometimes carries a CSS `transform` (the pull-down offset, the page-slide animation), and any computed transform on an ancestor silently breaks a `position: fixed` descendant's containing block (the same footgun `PageTransition`'s own comment documents for `BetslipBar`). `BottomNavSpacer` (just a conditional `pb-24`, not `fixed`) reserves scroll room for it and *is* safe to nest inside those wrappers. The `+` sheet's betting-off check fails fast into a `Modal` whose copy depends on *why* betting's blocked (`GroupBettingStatus.reason`, computed in `app/(app)/layout.tsx` from each group's latest season row, any status): `owner_off` for a continuous group the owner hasn't enabled (or a seasons-enabled group whose active season hasn't had betting opened yet) reuses the original fixed copy; `season_intermission` and `season_winding_down` are new, naming the owner ("Once @owner continues the group...") or the season respectively — `app/(app)/layout.tsx` used to only fetch each group's *active* season row, which meant a seasons-enabled group with no active season (intermission or winding_down) collapsed into the same "owner hasn't turned betting on" copy as a group that genuinely had it off; fetching the latest row regardless of status is what makes the three cases distinguishable. Picking a market type there hands off to `/groups/{id}/markets/new?type=...`, and since the drawer has already asked that question the wizard on the other side has no type picker at all (`initialMarketType` is now the market's type, not a pre-selection; a direct hit on the route with no `?type=` falls back to `yes_no`). Inbox's four "waiting on you" buckets (needs endorsement, awaiting vote, awaiting resolution, needs clarification) weren't re-homed anywhere cross-group when it was deleted — three of the four were always visible per-group already (`GroupFeedPage`'s status buckets), and the clarification one still shows as the market card's "!" badge. **This was in fact missed in practice, and `lib/tasks.ts` brought back the smallest useful piece of it** (see the tasks paragraph above): the two buckets that are genuinely blocked on one specific person, surfaced as a per-group card, a per-row count, and a Home-tab red dot. What deliberately did *not* come back is the Inbox *page* — there's still no cross-group destination that aggregates work items, only counts that point you into the group they belong to.
- **Nudges got their own cron job, not a branch inside `expire_stale()`.** Every sweep in `expire_stale()` is load-bearing: markets closing, votes auto-finalizing, seasons archiving, groups being deleted. The two nudges are flavor. Putting them in the same function would mean a bad query in a marketing prompt could throw partway through and leave a season un-archived, so `send_nudges()` runs as `barbets-nudges` on its own 15-minute schedule with its own failure domain. The corollary is worth stating: **new sweeps default to a new job, and only join `expire_stale()` if they're genuinely part of the state machine.**
- **The two nudges decide "is there anything worth notifying about," never "who."** `send_nudges()` only picks groups and markets; the entire "not a subject, hasn't bet on this market, hasn't bet anywhere in this group for 7 days" rule lives in `get_event_recipients`, alongside every other recipient rule in the app. Splitting it the other way would have created a second place where "who can see this market" is decided, which is exactly the thing `is_market_visible()` exists to prevent.
- **The closing nudge's rate limit is per group, not per recipient.** One nudge per group per 24 hours, checked against `notification_events` itself. Per-recipient would be more precise, but the recipient set for two markets closing in the same group is the same lapsed-member set, so the group-level check gives the same answer and needs no per-user delivery log to maintain and prune.
- **A payout that exactly equals the stake is not a win worth quoting.** It can only happen on a one-sided market (nobody took the other side, so there are no losing stakes to split). The reveal ledger used to render it as "+0 won", which read as a bug, and the push quoted the stake as winnings, which overstated it. Both now say the call was right and the stake comes back. The check is `payout == stake`, not a separate flag, because the parimutuel formula makes those two facts the same fact. `_finalize_season`'s `biggest_single_win` (`20260907220000`) had the same gap for longer: a lopsided season could crown a stake-return as the season's "biggest win" in the recap, since it's just the largest `payout`-reason ledger row by amount with no comparison against what was staked. Fixed the same way, `l.amount > b.amount` via `ledger.bet_id`, except for payout rows with no `bet_id` (creator/endorser cuts, the season-end bonus split), which aren't a stake return by construction and count unconditionally.
- **Two editable percentages became one editable and one computed.** The universal-loss split used to ask for a creator % *and* an endorser %, which required a "these can't sum past 100" validation, a matching Postgres check constraint, and an error state in two forms. Removing the endorser's cut (it rewarded a one-tap role with no risk attached, and nobody ever tuned the number) left one real decision plus arithmetic, so the remainder is now displayed read-only rather than asked for, and the whole class of invalid input stopped existing. **When a second input is fully determined by the first, show it instead of collecting it.**
- **Group logos are static assets with a free-text key, not a Storage bucket or an enum.** The art is app-authored, identical for every group, and never user-supplied, so there is nothing to gate: `public/avatars/` plus `lib/avatars.ts` gets CDN caching, no bucket, no signed URLs, and no second access-control system (contrast `resolution-proofs`, which really is user-supplied and really does need mediating). `groups.avatar_key` stores plain text with only a shape check so that adding or retiring an avatar is a PNG and one line of TypeScript, with no migration, and a key the current build doesn't recognize quietly falls back to the initials tile instead of erroring.
- **The season-over state took over the group hub instead of staying a banner-plus-separate-page.** Before this, an ended season showed a honey banner on the group hub linking to `/intermission`, and Leaderboard/Awards kept showing "current season" framing that no longer meant anything once every market was settled. Since between seasons every market *is* settled, the markets tab has nothing left to be a feed of — the recap (champion, final table, highlights, the owner's Season N+1 setup or the member's own result) is the page now, and the archive shrinks to two rows that open `/seasons`, a season-scoped drill-down, rather than staying the default view with nothing in it. `/intermission` is now a one-line redirect rather than deleted outright, so an old bookmark or push-notification deep link still lands somewhere real.
- **`titles_snapshot` exists because titles are lifetime/live by design and were never going to become season-scoped.** `group_titles` has no `season_id` anywhere in `_recompute_group_titles`/`_upsert_risk_taker` — a title is "who's best across the group's whole history," and making it reset per season would be a different feature, not a bug fix. That's fine for a *currently held* title (Awards already just shows the live row), but it can't answer "did I lose a title this season" on its own, since a live-only table has no memory of who held what before. `_finalize_season` snapshotting `group_titles` into `season_results.snapshot.titles_snapshot` at the moment each season closes gives every *later* season's recap something to diff against — the group hub's "titles changed hands" and the Awards page's "lost this season" cards both just compare the live rows to the snapshot from *two* seasons back (the one taken when the season-before-last closed, i.e. the state at the start of the season that just ended), via `lib/seasonTitleDiff.ts`. A group's first-ever season end has nothing to diff against and that's treated as "no changes," not an error — the feature quietly does nothing until there's a second season-end to compare.
- **User avatars are a public Storage bucket, deliberately not the `resolution-proofs` private-bucket pattern, and a genuinely new precedent (not a copy of either existing one).** A proof photo is one image, viewed rarely, behind a real visibility check — a 60-second signed URL is fine there. An avatar renders in bulk, everywhere a nickname does (every leaderboard row, eventually every `@mention`), so re-minting a signed URL per avatar per render would be real, avoidable load for content that isn't actually sensitive. `avatars` is public, and the object path is deterministic (`{user_id}.jpg`, upserted on re-upload) rather than a stored `avatar_path` column — `users.avatar_updated_at` alone says whether one exists and doubles as the cache-busting `?v=` param, so there's nothing to keep in sync. **There is no image moderation anywhere in this codebase, and a public avatar is visible to the whole group with no review step.** That's an accepted risk consistent with this app's existing trust model (nicknames aren't verified either, and any member can already see every other member's real name-equivalent), not an oversight — revisit if the app ever grows past small, mutually-known friend groups.
- **Avatars are deliberately not yet threaded into `ShareRecordCard` or `RevealTicket`.** Both capture a DOM node to a PNG via `html-to-image` (see the reveal-page and share-image design notes below), which needs every image inside the captured node to be same-origin or explicitly CORS-safe — that's already why those components use a plain `<img>` and never `next/image`. A Supabase Storage avatar is a different origin from the app, and whether `html-to-image` can rasterize it cleanly (crossOrigin attribute, actual CORS headers on the object response) hasn't been verified. Rather than risk a silently blank or tainted-canvas avatar on a feature that already works (the shareable record card, the reveal ticket), avatar rendering for this first pass is limited to surfaces `html-to-image` never touches: the leaderboard and `/profile/account`. Verify the cross-origin capture behavior before adding an avatar to either captured card.
- **Dropping the "BB-" invite prefix rewrote every existing code rather than grandfathering them.** Two live formats would mean every input, paste handler, and support conversation has to know about both forever. Old links keep working through one normalizer (`lib/inviteCode.ts`) applied at the three entry points every join funnels through, so no Postgres function ever learns that the old format existed, and the tolerance can be deleted in one place once printed cards age out.
- **`season_optins` and `season_optouts` are two separate tables on purpose, not one table with inverted meaning.** The obvious design once seasons flipped to opt-out-by-default would be to rename `season_optins` and read "no row" as "included." That breaks `_cleanup_departing_member()` (shared by `leave_group`/`remove_member`), which already deletes a departing member's opt row on every departure — under an inverted single table, that delete would silently *un-exclude* them instead of excluding them, auto-reseeding someone who explicitly left. Keeping both tables (`season_optins`: a dormant member asking in; `season_optouts`: an active member asking out) means the existing departure cleanup, `join_group`, and `opt_in_season` needed zero changes — `start_season()`'s reseed rule is just a union of the two default populations.
- **Both create flows are wizards whose first question was already asked by the bottom nav's drawer.** `/groups/new` and `/groups/{id}/markets/new` are the two routes `shouldHideBottomNav()` blanks the tab bar for, and they now each own their whole screen: no `PageHeader`, a step bar (back caret, progress pills, `1 of 2` / `ASK`) and one question per step, with the primary action pinned to the bottom by `min-h-[calc(100dvh-4rem)]` plus `mt-auto` rather than a fixed footer. The point isn't the steps, it's that **the drawer's answers are carried forward as context instead of re-asked**: create-group shows the name and token allocation as a dark ticket with an Edit pill (which opens the same two fields inline, so a typo doesn't mean backing out of the flow), and create-market drops its type segmented control entirely because `?type=` already decided it. Step state is local to the client component — no route per step — so back/forward inside a wizard costs nothing and a half-finished market can't be linked to.

  Two mechanics make that work. **Every route into creating a group goes through the drawer**, including the groups hub's own "Start a group" button, which dispatches a `barbets:new-group` window event that `BottomNav` listens for rather than linking to `/groups/new` — an event and not a search param because there's exactly one listener, it owns state nothing else should set directly, and a half-open drawer isn't worth linking to or restoring on back. And because each step is a screenful with a pinned footer, both wizards `window.scrollTo(0, 0)` on every step change: without it the browser restores the previous scroll position and the flow arrives with its own step bar above the fold. The pinning uses `--flow-height` (`globals.css`), which is `100dvh` minus the layout's in-flow `env(safe-area-inset-top)` padding — sized against a plain `100dvh` the page overflows the viewport by exactly that inset, which is what made these screens scroll a notch even when everything fitted. It's a custom property rather than a Tailwind arbitrary value because `calc(100dvh - env(safe-area-inset-top))` can't safely be written as one: the hyphens inside the `env()` keyword are indistinguishable from the subtraction operator to an arbitrary-value parser.

  Fitting the viewport had a second-order consequence worth remembering: **`PullToRefresh` arms on "scrolled to the very top," and a screen that never scrolls is at the top permanently**, so every downward drag anywhere on the form pulled the refresh indicator. Both wizards therefore opt out via the same `isCreateFlow()` predicate that hides the bottom bar — appropriate anyway, since a wizard's answers live in client state and its only server data is the group and member list it was handed on entry, so there is nothing a refresh could fetch. (`router.refresh()` would in fact have preserved the in-progress answers — it merges an updated RSC payload without discarding unaffected client state — so this is about a gesture that fires constantly and achieves nothing, not about losing work.)
- **Create-group's advanced settings are a separate view with a label-only entry row.** The row says "Advanced settings" and nothing else: listing endorsement/hedging/payout/challenge-window under it would make a first-time group owner feel they had four decisions to make, when the honest answer is that the defaults suit a small group and everything stays editable from Settings. The view itself deliberately uses **the same names and the same copy as the group's own Settings page** — "Hedging", "Split universal losses", Creator % editable with Open markets % shown read-only beside it — rather than friendlier one-off labels, because these are the same four settings seen twice within a few minutes, and a member who renamed them in their head at creation would have to re-learn them the first time they opened Settings. There's no "reset to defaults": the view opens on the defaults, so the button only ever undoes deliberate work.
- **A season name is collected at create time but named afterwards, through `nameActiveSeason()`.** `create_group()` brings the first season into existence, so the name has nowhere to go until it returns. Rather than widen the function's signature (and the migration, and the `DROP FUNCTION` dance that a changed signature requires), `lib/actions/seasons.ts` gained a thin action that looks up the group's `active` season and falls through to the existing `rename_season()` RPC. It's fired best-effort alongside `updateGroupSettings()`/`setGroupAvatar()` after the group already exists, so a failure leaves a group named "Season 1" rather than no group at all.
- **Both horizontal rails need `scroll-pl-5` to match their `px-5`, and it isn't optional.** The "Open Bets" rail on `/profile` and the awards shelf both break the page gutter (`-mx-5 … px-5`) so cards can bleed to the screen edge while still starting flush with the heading above them. With `scroll-snap-type: x mandatory`, though, the browser snaps on first layout: `scroll-snap-align: start` aligns a card to the scrollport's *border* edge, so it immediately scrolls the 20px of padding away and parks every card 20px left of the text it sits under. `scroll-padding-left` is what tells snapping where the content actually starts. The cards are also sized `calc((100% - 10px) / 2)` rather than a fixed pixel width so exactly two fit a phone screen — which is why the awards rail's dot indicator measures its first child instead of dividing by a hard-coded step.
- **The market subject picker became a chip strip, and "Nobody" became a real chip.** A typeahead is the right control for a list you can't hold in your head; a group small enough to bet in is small enough to show every member at once, and "who is this about?" is a question you answer by recognising a name, not recalling one. Making "Nobody" a selectable chip rather than the absence of a selection is the load-bearing half: it separates "I decided this isn't about anyone" from "I haven't got to that field yet," which the empty typeahead couldn't distinguish. The subject cap (`totalMemberCount - 2`) is enforced by disabling the unselected chips rather than by a message. Chips are alphabetical (membership order is an order nobody can predict) and paged at 20, with **anyone already selected pinned into view regardless of which page they fall on** — paging away someone's own choice reads as having lost it, which is the failure mode any "show more" list has to design against.
- **Every pre-group screen is full bleed on paper, with no `Card`.** The splash, both auth modes, forgot/reset password, the invite flow, 404 and offline used to be a centered `max-w-sm` column with the form inside a `<Card>` — a `paper-white` box on a `paper` page, which bought a border and a shadow but no actual separation, since there was nothing else on the screen to separate from. They're now single-purpose screens: a header row (back caret + coin), a 34px headline, and one honey CTA. `Card` is retained for surfaces that really do sit among other content (the sign-up terms consent, the invite's group card, the offline balances box), which is the distinction worth keeping — **a card earns its border by having neighbours.**

  Two supporting pieces came out of it. `components/ui/Field.tsx` is the underlined text field that replaced the boxed `inputClasses` string previously copy-pasted verbatim into `AuthForms`, `ForgotPasswordForm` and `JoinFlow`; its label sits above the input but *after* it in the DOM (`flex-col-reverse`) purely so focus styling is a `peer-focus:` variant rather than a client component holding an `isFocused` boolean. And `Button` gained an `xl` size for the one CTA on these screens, which moved `font-semibold` out of the shared base and into each size entry — `cn()` is a plain join, not a tailwind-merge, so two `font-*` utilities on one element would have resolved by stylesheet order rather than by intent.
- **The splash's "I have an invite code" button routes through the existing sign-in bounce rather than previewing the group pre-auth.** `/join` (no code) is a code-entry screen that does no lookup of its own: it pushes to `/join/[code]`, which already redirects a signed-out visitor to `/login?next=/join/XXXX` and lands them back on the invite once they have an account. This note used to justify that by saying letting `get_group_by_invite_code` run for `anon` was "technically available but widens who can probe codes" — at the time that was written, `anon` genuinely *could* call it (see the entry on the `anon` grant below), so this wasn't a hypothetical being avoided, it was the actual live hole the revoke later closed. `get_group_by_invite_code` is now explicitly revoked from `anon`, so this UI choice and the database are both enforcing the same thing rather than one being a courtesy on top of the other. `invite_code_exists()` is the actual sanctioned `anon` touchpoint for exactly the question a pre-auth preview would need answered ("is this code real"), without handing back the group's name. `InviteCodeBoxes` gained a `tone` prop for the paper ground instead of being forked, so the 4-character format and the legacy-prefix paste handling stay in one component.
- **The offline screen's CTA is the one outline button in the app, deliberately.** Every other screen's single action is a honey fill. "Try again" is the only action the app cannot promise will do anything, and dressing it identically to actions that always work is a small lie told on the screen least able to absorb one. `OfflineGroupBalances` likewise carries one caption for the whole box ("Saved before you went offline, so these may be out of date") rather than a per-row age: every figure there is stale by construction, and the exact minute count is not what it's read for.
- **The groups hub is one card per group, and a lifted shadow is the only "needs you" signal.** Hairline-divided rows in a single container made every group look like an item in a list of settings; a card per group makes each one a place. A group with tasks gets `shadow-sm` and a dark/honey avatar tile, one without gets neither — the difference is legible without spending a second accent colour on it, and the red dot next to "N need you" is still the literal answer. Groups in intermission drop to a smaller `paper-dim` tier below the active ones (the existing stable sort already partitioned them) and lose their net-tokens figure, which is stale the moment nothing is being wagered. The hub's caption counts only the active tier, since a group between seasons isn't a table you can sit down at right now.
- **Awards leads with the count, and the titles you hold are a shelf rather than a stack.** Holding three titles used to mean three full-width dark hero cards, which pushed everyone else's titles below the fold and made "I hold three of eight" (now four, since Bandwagon/Cursed/On Fire/Whale were cut — see the Awards entry above) something you had to work out by scrolling. The page now opens with that sentence, then a horizontal snap rail of full-width cards, one per swipe, each also carrying the title's description (`AwardsRail`, a client component only because the dot indicator tracks scroll position), then the titles other people hold as ordinary rows, then one collapsed row for the unclaimed ones. The "Propose an award / Soon" placeholder was removed rather than restyled: a disabled row promising a feature is a cost paid on every visit for information that only needed saying once.
- **The group header lost its "My bets" shortcut, and the route it pointed at is deliberately left in place.** `/groups/{id}/bets` still renders and is still linkable; nothing points at it from the UI any more. The header had two icon chips fighting for the same corner, and the one that mattered (settings for an owner, group info for everyone else) was losing — it's now a single 44px control. Your own open positions are already answered better by `/profile`'s "Open Bets" rail, which spans every group rather than just this one. If the route turns out to be missed, the fix is a link, not a rebuild.
- **The leaderboard's hero states the gap, not just the order.** The bar rows answer "who's ahead"; they don't answer "by how much, and am I catching up," which is the question anyone in second actually opens the page for. The hero puts the leader's balance and seven-day direction of travel above three figures — your rank, your tokens, and the gap — and flips the third from "behind the leader" to "clear of 2nd" when you're the one in front, since a zero gap says nothing. The seven-day figure is one extra `ledger` query scoped to the leader's own membership; everything else in the hero was already loaded for the rows underneath it.
- **The QR invite encodes the real join URL, not a `/go/`-style redirect, and the OS decides whether the app is installed.** The group hub's "Show QR" pill (`InviteQrButton`, rendered client-side by the `qrcode` package into a data URL, white full-screen, with a best-effort screen wake lock) encodes `https://app.mybarbets.com/join/<code>?src=qr`. Two designs were on the table: a server redirect like `/go/[batch]` that sniffs the UA and sends everyone to a store, or the join link itself. A redirect can never know whether the app is installed, so it would send an existing user to the store page of an app they already have; the OS *can* know, via App Links / Universal Links, and when those verify it opens the installed app before the browser ever loads the URL. So the URL is the plain join link, the native shells claim it (`AndroidManifest.xml` `autoVerify` intent-filter, `App.entitlements` `applinks:app.mybarbets.com`, `public/.well-known/assetlinks.json` + `apple-app-site-association`), `NativeDeepLink` turns the open into an in-app navigation (handling both `appUrlOpen` for a warm open and `App.getLaunchUrl()` for an Android cold start, which the App plugin never raises as an event; a `sessionStorage` stamp stops the launch URL re-navigating on every remount), and the not-installed case falls through to the browser, where `/join/[code]` completes the join directly (there's no gate in front of it any more, see "The post-join open-the-app nudge") and `OpenAppPrompt` becomes the deferred deep link afterward. Details worth keeping straight:
  - **The claim is `/join/*` only, never the whole host.** `app.mybarbets.com` also serves the emailed password-reset and signup-confirm links, and those *must* keep opening in a browser, a one-time-token link has no in-app equivalent. Claiming the host would have hijacked them into the app.
  - **The deferred half reuses the printed-card pattern and nothing else.** Android: the store link carries `referrer=utm_source=qr&utm_medium=invite&invite_code=XXXX`, and the local `DeferredInvite` plugin reads it back through Google's own Play Install Referrer client (`com.android.installreferrer:installreferrer`, first-party, no attribution SDK). iOS has no install referrer at all; the store link's `ct=invite-XXXX` is an App Analytics campaign token the app cannot read, so `OpenAppPrompt` copies the invite URL to the clipboard on the store tap and the iOS plugin reads the pasteboard on first open. That read is fenced twice: `detectPatterns(.probableWebURL)` first, which checks the shape without reading or prompting, and only within 24 hours of install (the app container's creation date), because a programmatic pasteboard read shows the system "paste from Safari" prompt on iOS 16+, which is fine on a brand-new install a scan just led to and not fine as a surprise on an old one. `DeferredInviteLink` stamps `localStorage` *before* the native call so the whole thing runs at most once per install, including the first open after this ships on every existing install (which finds nothing).
  - **Placeholders that have to be filled in before any of this verifies:** `assetlinks.json` needs the SHA-256 of the *Play App Signing* certificate (Play Console > App signing, not the upload key), `apple-app-site-association` needs the Team ID prefix, and Associated Domains has to be switched on for the App ID in the Apple Developer portal. Until then a scan just opens the browser join flow, same as any other invite link without a verified App Link. Not verified on a device by the author; the PR's manual test steps are the checklist.
  - **`?src=` is a lifecycle tag, not a routing signal.** Nothing behaves differently for `qr` vs `code` vs a bare link; it exists so `lifecycle_events.metadata->>'source'` can say which of the three actually brings people in. `InviteCodeBoxes` also learned to read a pasted invite *link* (`inviteCodeFromText`, which finds `/join/XXXX` before `normalizeInviteCode` gets a chance to turn "https" into a code), since the clipboard now sometimes holds one.
- **Curated market-template rows are authored directly in Supabase Studio's table editor, not through an app-facing function.** The ask was "a database table, editable without a code deploy" — which is already fully satisfied with zero app code, since Studio's table editor connects with a privileged Postgres role that bypasses RLS entirely, the same way the zero-policy `app_admins` table is only ever edited from the dashboard or `service_role`. Building an `is_platform_admin()`-gated CRUD function for curated rows now, with no caller anywhere in the app, would be exactly the failure class this doc already warns about elsewhere: an RPC that sits unexercised until some future admin UI grows a reason to call it, at which point nobody would trust it works without testing it again from scratch. `/admin` itself is also mid-migration to a separate repo (`barbets-admin`, see "Platform admin" above) not in this working directory, so adding a new admin capability to the code half slated for deletion would be wasted motion. This is a one-way door only in the sense that it's a starting point, not a constraint: an admin-gated `create_curated_market_template()` following `get_platform_admin_stats()`'s exact gate shape is a small follow-up whenever a real curation workflow actually wants one.
- **The `@`-placeholder heuristic for a freshly-composed `yes_no`/`over_under` market is scoped to the title only, and is always shown back to the saver rather than trusted silently.** Turning "Will Jake finish his drink first?" plus a selected subject into a reusable "Will @ finish his drink first?" template means finding the subject's own name inside free text that was never structured for it — unlike a `multiple_choice` option, which is either a clean `@nickname` mention or it isn't, with nothing to guess at. The heuristic (a case-insensitive whole-word match) can miss (a title that says "they" instead of a name) or, in principle, match the wrong thing, so `SaveAsTemplateModal` always surfaces its result in an editable field with a "save without the swap" escape hatch before committing, rather than silently deciding it got it right. Scoped to the title and not the description too, to keep a heuristic that's already a guess from compounding across two fields at once. A save is never blocked by an unclean case (zero or multiple subjects selected, or no literal match) — it just saves with no placeholder, which is a fine outcome, not a failure.
